Typing Fallback Functions: A Semantic Approach to Type Safe Smart Contracts DOI: XXXXXXX.XXXXXXX Conference: ???; ???; ??? ISBN: 978-1-4503-XXXX-X/2018/06
Stian Lybech, Daniele Gorla, Luca Aceto

Abstract
. This paper develops semantic typing in a smart-contract setting to ensure type safety of code that uses statically untypable language constructs, such as the fallback function. The idea is that the creator of a contract on the blockchain equips code containing such constructs with a formal proof of its type safety, given in terms of the semantics of types. Then, a user of the contract only needs to check the validity of the provided ‘proof certificate’ of type safety. This is a form of proof-carrying code, which naturally fits with the immutable nature of the blockchain environment. As a concrete application of our approach, we focus on ensuring information flow control and non-interference for \TINYSOL , a distilled version of the Solidity language, through security types. We provide the semantics of types in terms of a typed operational semantics of \TINYSOL , and a way for expressing the proofs of safety as coinductively-defined typing interpretations and for representing them compactly via up-to techniques, similar to those used for bisimilarity. We also show how our machinery can be used to type the typical pointer-to-implementation pattern based on the fallback function. However, our main contribution is not the safety theorem per se (and so security properties different from non-interference can be considered as well), but rather the presentation of the theoretical developments necessary to make this approach work in a blockchain/smart-contract setting.



1.
Introduction

In 2017, a vulnerability in a smart contract on the Ethereum platform led to the theft of approximately 31 million dollars’ worth of Ether (see
[
28
]
for a code example with comments explaining the details of the attack).
This vulnerability, known as the
Parity Multisig Wallet attack
(PMW), involved an intricate interplay between two peculiar, low-level features of the Solidity smart-contract language
[
19
]
:
delegate calls
and
fallback functions
, which can be used for developing utility libraries.
The root cause was an unintended and unwanted flow of data from an untrusted source to a trusted variable, which was used by an attacker to access wallet contracts and drain them of their Ether.
In security parlance, the
integrity
of that variable was compromised.
Preserving integrity is crucial to guarantee
non-interference

[
21
]
and, in
[
48
]
, Volpano, Irvine and Smith created a type system for ensuring such a property, using the concept of
security types
—that is, types that control the information flow between memory segments of different security levels.
This line of work has been taken up in

[
2
;
24
]
, where type systems with security types for a Solidity-like language have been devised
to prevent such unwanted flows.
Unfortunately, the language used in
[
2
]
does not include the fallback function construct. Moreover, despite using the Parity Multisig Wallet attack as their motivating example, the authors of the type system in
[
24
]
do not actually provide a typing rule for the fallback function, which was a key factor in enabling the aforementioned vulnerability.

This is unsurprising, because the fallback function enables a limited form of
reflection
, or at least introspection, which is notoriously difficult to handle using a syntactic type system.
The source of this problem is that the fallback function is invoked when a non-existing method is called, and the body of the fallback function can then use information about the failed call to determine how the computation shall proceed.
This information is only available at runtime, hence it cannot be assigned a static type able to distinguish between the safe and unsafe usages of this construct.

The difficulty in handling the fallback function is a prime example of a more general shortcoming of the standard, syntactic approach to type soundness
[
49
]
, namely that it tends to equate ‘well-typedness’ (a syntactic property) with ‘safety’ (a semantic, or behavioural, property).
Specifically, in the syntactic approach, one defines a syntactic predicate, well-typedness, by means of syntax-driven type rules.
These rules are defined
a priori
and then subsequently shown to
imply
safety, but they are not
derived
from the notion of safety itself.
This makes the syntactic approach incapable of reasoning about programs or constructs that behave safely at runtime, but for which the difference between safe and unsafe usages is not a purely syntactic property.
Examples of such constructs include inline assembly, pointer arithmetics, reflection, and, indeed, the fallback function.

This issue was originally noted in
[
29
;
16
]
and, more recently, by
Caires in
[
13
]
and Timany et al. in
[
47
]
, who instead advocate a different approach, known as the
semantic approach to type soundness
, or just
semantic typing
for short, which is capable of reasoning about even seemingly ‘unsafe’ programming constructs.
Semantic typing is now a well established technique and, among other properties, it has also been used to ensure non-interference (see, e.g.,
[
22
;
18
;
39
]
). However,
as far as we know, the semantic approach to type soundness has not hitherto been applied in the setting of blockchains and smart contracts, even though it seems particularly well-suited for this purpose.
Suppose a contract makes use of a syntactically untypable construct, such as a fallback function, but does it in such a way that it can be shown to preserve type safety via a formal proof, given in terms of the semantics of types.
Such a proof can then be supplied along with the code of the contract containing the fallback function, when it is declared on the blockchain, as a form of proof-carrying code
[
33
]
.
The immutable nature of the blockchain environment ensures that neither the code nor the proof can be subsequently altered.
Thus, as long as the proof can be verified by any user of that contract, the untypable fragment of code does not itself need to be type checked using the syntactic rules.
Of course,
finding
such a proof may then be challenging, but this obligation now falls on the contract creator, rather than on the user of the contract, who only needs to check the validity of the provided ‘proof certificate’ of type safety.
In this way, we may not be able to make the fallback function itself typable by syntactic means, but we can provide a method for reasoning about, and ensuring, the type safety of contracts
using
that construct.

Our main contribution is the presentation of the theoretical developments necessary to make this approach work in a blockchain/smart-contract setting.
Similar to
[
24
]
, we shall focus on ensuring non-interference by means of types for secure information flows in the style of Volpano, Irvine and Smith
[
48
]
, yet this should only be viewed as an example of how the semantic approach may be applied (indeed, in Appendix
we show the changes needed to ensure
call integrity
, a property designed in
[
23
]
to avoid attacks similar to PMW).
To keep the presentation simple, with a focus on the fallback function, we shall use the language
TinySol

[
6
]
, which models some of the core features of the smart-contract language Solidity.
A syntactic version of the Volpano-Irvine-Smith type system has already been developed for a version of
TinySol

without
fallback functions in
[
2
]
, so we can adapt this work by adding just the fallback function construct.
We present this extended version of
TinySol
in Section
2.1
, and review the language of security types in Section
2.2
.

There are different approaches to defining a semantic model of types and type judgments.
One is the step-indexed model of Appel and McAllester
[
4
]
, which was further extended by Ahmed in
[
3
]
.
This approach has been applied in a variety of settings, for example to reason about the safety of ‘unsafe code’ in core Rust libraries
[
25
]
; see
[
47
]
for an overview of work based on this approach.
Another approach is based on
typing interpretations
, which are coinductively defined objects corresponding to fragments of transition systems.
It is reminiscent of the logical relations technique of Plotkin
[
36
]
and Tait
[
46
]
, and it is the method used by Caires in
[
13
]
for a simple type system for the
\pi
-calculus
[
32
]
.
Informally, a typing interpretation for a syntactic category, e.g. programs
P
,
is a
\Gamma
-indexed family of sets of programs
\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma}
, where
\Gamma
is a collection of type predicates, defined such that
P\in\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma}
implies that
(1)
P
satisfies the type predicates in
\Gamma
, and
(2) If
P\rightarrow P^{\prime}
then
P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma}
.
The largest typing interpretation for a given
\Gamma
is the union of all typing interpretations for
\Gamma
, written
\nu\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma}
, similar to bisimilarity being the union of all bisimulations.
The typing interpretation

\nu\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma}
is the
typing
of
\Gamma
, and the semantic typing relation
\Gamma\vDash P
is then defined as
P\in\nu\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma}
.
Proving semantic type safety of
P
w.r.t. a given
\Gamma
thus becomes a matter of finding a typing interpretation for
\Gamma
that contains
P
.
By following Caires’ approach, the proofs of type safety for unsafe code that have to be stored on the blockchain are simply the typing interpretations themselves.

In Section
3
, we define the semantics of types in terms of a
typed operational semantics
of
TinySol
, given in Section
3.1
.
It is based on an (untyped) small-step operational semantics of the language (given in Appendix
A
) with type constraints inspired from the syntactic type system. This ensures a crucial property, which is needed for the definition of typing interpretations:
a non-terminal, stuck configuration indicates a type error
.
In a sense, our approach may resemble
gradual typing

[
14
;
44
]
, which allows a mixing of static and dynamic typing.
However, the crucial difference is that our typed operational semantics is defined solely for the purpose of giving a semantics to types.
The runtime semantics of
TinySol
is still untyped, and our typing interpretations are intended to
statically
witness the safety of statically untypable code, without runtime type related checks.

The task of finding a typing interpretation falls on the contract creator, but any client
using
a contract with such an associated proof must, of course, also be able to verify that it indeed is a typing interpretation.
Both tasks can be difficult, since typing interpretations can be large and, in limit cases, also infinite.
This also threatens the practical applicability of our proposed approach, since infinite objects cannot be stored on the blockchain, unless they have a finite representation.
Inspired by the similarity between typing interpretations and bisimulations, we therefore adapt the work of Pous and Sangiorgi
[
37
]
on enhancements of the bisimulation proof-technique, and propose
up-to techniques for typing interpretations
, which may be used to reduce their size.
The benefits of this approach and its soundness are discussed in Section
4
.
To our knowledge, this is the first use of up-to techniques in a predicative (i.e., non-relational) setting; indeed, traditionally these techniques have been used to reduce the size of (inductively or coinductively defined)
equivalences
on programs (see, e.g.,
[
8
;
9
;
15
;
17
;
41
]
), whereas typing interpretations are (unary)
predicates
on programs.
The possibility of exploiting up-to techniques is a key advantage of giving the semantics to our types by means of typing interpretations (instead of, e.g., logical relations
[
26
;
27
]
).

Finally, we conclude the paper in Section
5
with a discussion of some avenues for future research.
All proofs and many definitions are deferred to the appendices for space reasons. A synopsis of all notations in this paper is in Appendix
.

2.
The
TinySol
language and its security types

2.1.
Typed Syntax

We use an extended version of the
TinySol
language, based on the presentation in
[
2
]
, which, in turn, is based on the original presentation in
[
6
]
.
Its syntax is given in Figure
1
, where the notation
\mathop{\ \widetilde{\cdot}\ }
denotes (possibly empty) sequences of elements.
The syntax consists of declarations for contracts
DC
, fields
DF
, and methods
DM
, followed by values
v
, expressions
e
, and statements
S
.
A contract contains declarations of fields and methods, and of the so-called
fallback function
(discussed later).
The set of
values

\mathcal{V}
, ranged over by
v
, consists of integers
\mathbb{Z}
, ranged over by
z
, booleans
\mathbb{B}=\left\{\,\text{T},\text{F}\,\right\}
, ranged over by
b
, method names
\mathcal{N}_{M}
, ranged over by
f
,
1
1

1

Having method names as values is related to the fallback function and the special variable
id
—see later on.

and address names
\mathcal{N}_{A}
, ranged over by
X,Y
.
Note that we use a
typed
syntax, with type annotations
I_{s}
and
\text{var}(B_{s})
appearing in declarations of contracts and of local variables; these will be described in Section
2.2
.

\begin{array}[]{r @{~} c @{~} l @{\qquad} l}DC\in\mathcal{D}_{C}&::=&\epsilon~~\big|~~~\texttt{contract $X$ : $I_{s}$ \lx@text@lbrace }&&\qquad\ \ \ \texttt{field balance := $z$; $DF$}&&\qquad\ \ \ \texttt{func send() \lx@text@lbrace skip \lx@text@rbrace $DM$}&&\qquad\ \ \ \texttt{func fallback() \lx@text@lbrace $S$ \lx@text@rbrace }&&\quad\ \texttt{\lx@text@rbrace $DC$}DF\in\mathcal{D}_{F}&::=&\epsilon~~\big|~~~\texttt{field p := $v$;}DFDM\in\mathcal{D}_{M}&::=&\epsilon~~\big|~~~\texttt{func $f(\widetilde{x})$ \lx@text@lbrace $S$ \lx@text@rbrace }DMv\in\mathcal{V}&::=&\mathbb{Z}\cup\mathbb{B}\cup\mathcal{N}_{A}\cup\mathcal{N}_{M}e\in\text{Exp}&::=&v~~\big|~~~x~~\big|~~~\texttt{$e$.balance}~~\big|~~~\texttt{$e$.$p$}~~\big|~~~\op(\widetilde{e})~~\big|~~~\texttt{this}~~\big|~~~\texttt{sender}~~\big|~~~\texttt{value}~~\big|~~~\texttt{id}~~\big|~~~\texttt{args}S\in\text{Stm}&::=&\texttt{skip}~~\big|~~~\texttt{throw}~~\big|~~~\texttt{$\text{var}(B_{s})$ $x$ := $e$ in $S$}~~\big|~~~\texttt{$x$ := $e$}~~\big|~~~\texttt{this.$p$ := $e$}~~\big|~~~\texttt{$S_{1}$;$S_{2}$}&~~\big|&\texttt{if $e$ then $S_{\text{T}}$ else $S_{\text{F}}$ }~~\big|~~~\texttt{while $e$ do $S$}~~\big|~~~\texttt{call $e_{1}$.$m$($\widetilde{e}$)\$$e_{2}$}~~\big|~~~\texttt{dcall $e$.$m$($\widetilde{e}$)}\end{array}

where
x,y\in\mathcal{N}_{V}
(variable names),
p,q\in\mathcal{N}_{F}
(field names),
X,Y\in\mathcal{N}_{A}
(address names),

f\in\mathcal{N}_{M}
(method names),
m\in\left\{\,\texttt{id}\,\right\}\cup\mathcal{N}_{M}
,
I\in\mathcal{N}_{I}
(interface names), and
s\in\mathcal{S}
(security levels).

Figure 1.
The syntax of
TinySol
.

There are six ‘magic’ keywords appearing in the syntax:

•

balance
, an integer field that records the current balance of the contract;
it can be read from, but not directly assigned to, except through method calls.

•

value
, an integer variable that is bound to the currency amount transferred with a call.

•

sender
, an address variable that is bound to the address of the caller of a method.

•

this
, an address variable that is bound to the address of the contract that contains the method that is currently executed.

•

id
and
args
, two variables that, when the fallback function is invoked, contain the name of the (non-existing) called method and the list of actual arguments.

All except
balance
are local variables, and we collectively refer to them as ‘magic variables’.

The core of the language is the declaration of the expressions
e
and of the statements
S
, which are similar to those for the classic imperative language
While

[
34
]
, except for the aforementioned magic keywords, and for the addition of method calls, which have three flavours:

•

In an ordinary method call
call
\!\!e_{1}
.
f
(
\widetilde{e}
)$
e_{2}
, expression
e_{1}
must evaluate to a contract address
X
and
e_{2}
must evaluate to an integer
z
.
The latter is the amount of currency transferred from the caller to the callee along with the call:
this amount will be deducted from the caller’s
balance
and added to the callee’s
balance
. All currency transfers are done through method calls, and all contracts therefore contain a standard
send()
method (for
send

\in\mathcal{N}_{M}
), which does nothing, apart from ensuring that it is always able to receive payments.

•

In a delegate call
dcall
\!\!e
.
f
(
\widetilde{e}
)
, the method body
S
is executed in the context of the caller, rather than the callee.
This means that the magic variables
this
,
sender
and
value
are not rebound, so the method executes
as if
it were declared in the caller contract.
Hence, there is also no currency transfer, since the execution is local.
The purpose of this feature is to facilitate code reuse and allow for the development of library contracts (see, e.g., Figure
7
).

•

Differently from
[
2
;
6
]
and similarly to Solidity
[
19
]
, if a call
call
\!\!X
.
f
(
\widetilde{v}
)$
z
is issued to a contract
X
that does
not
contain a method named
f
, then the fallback function of
X
will be executed.
In this case, the magic variables
id
and args contain, respectively, the method name and the actual arguments of the failed method call, i.e.
f
and
\widetilde{v}
, which allow the programmer to perform some error handling, like forwarding the call to another contract
Y
:

⬇

func

fallback
()

{

call

Y
.
id
(
args
)
$value

}

Hence, we use
m
instead of
f
in the syntax for method calls and delegate calls in Figure
1
to allow both
id
and a method name
f
be used for invocations.

To give an operational semantics for
TinySol
, the first thing to do is turning all declarations into some environments, to record the bindings of variables, fields, methods, and contracts.
Such environments are modelled as the following sets of partial functions:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\lx@intercol\text{env}_{V}\in\text{Env}_{V}:(\mathcal{N}_{V}\cup\left\{\,\texttt{value},\texttt{sender},\texttt{this},\texttt{id},\texttt{args}\,\right\})\rightharpoonup\mathcal{V}\hfil\lx@intercol\vskip 5.69046pt\\
\text{env}_{S}\in\text{Env}_{S}:\mathcal{N}_{A}\rightharpoonup\text{Env}_{F}&\text{env}_{F}\in\text{Env}_{F}:(\mathcal{N}_{F}\cup\left\{\,\texttt{balance}\,\right\})\rightharpoonup\mathcal{V}\\
\text{env}_{T}\in\text{Env}_{T}:\mathcal{N}_{A}\rightharpoonup\text{Env}_{M}&\text{env}_{M}\in\text{Env}_{M}:(\mathcal{N}_{M}\cup\left\{\,\texttt{fallback}\,\right\})\rightharpoonup(\mathcal{N}_{V}^{*}\times\text{Stm})\end{array}

We regard each environment
\text{env}_{J}
, for any
J\in\left\{\,V,F,M,S,T\,\right\}
, as a (finite) set of pairs
(d,c)
where
d\in\dom\left(\text{env}_{J}\right)
and
c\in\codom\left(\text{env}_{J}\right)
.
The notation
\text{env}_{J},d:c
denotes the extension of
\text{env}_{J}
with the new pair
(d,c)
; the update of
\text{env}_{J}
where
d
is mapped to
c
is denoted as
\text{env}_{J}\kern-2.0pt\left[d\mapsto c\right]
; updates are left-associative.
We write
\text{env}_{J}^{\emptyset}
for the empty
J
-environment.
To simplify the notation, when two or more environments appear together, we shall use the convention of writing the subscripts together (e.g. we write
\text{env}_{MF}
instead of
\text{env}_{M},\text{env}_{F}
).

The
method table

\text{env}_{T}
and the
state

\text{env}_{S}
map addresses to method and field environments, respectively.
Thus, for each contract, we have the list of methods it declares (together with the lists of formal parameters and the statement that forms their bodies) and its current state (its fields and their values).
The method table is constant, once all declarations are performed;
in contrast, the state will change during the evaluation of a program.
Lastly, we also have a
variable environment

\text{env}_{V}
, which contains bindings for locally declared variables, including the magic variables and formal parameters of methods;
hence, this too will change during the execution of a program.

Unlike the presentation in
[
2
]
and similarly to
[
1
]
, we rely on a small-step operational semantics, and we therefore also introduce
stacks
(that, again, use type information—see Section
2.2
):

\begin{array}[]{r @{~} c @{~} l @{\qquad} l}Q\in\mathcal{Q}&::=&\bot~~\big|~~~S;Q~~\big|~~~\text{del}(x);Q~~\big|~~~(\text{env}_{V},\Delta);Q~~\big|~~~s;Q\end{array}

Briefly,
\bot
is the empty stack, and besides statements
S
, a stack can also contain: the end-of-scope symbol
\text{del}(x)
, denoting deallocation of a local variable
x
; the method-return symbol
(\text{env}_{V},\Delta)
, where
\text{env}_{V}
contains bindings for local variables and
\Delta
contains their type annotations, which were in use before the method call and have to be restored at its completion; and, lastly, a security level
s
, since the current security level may be changed and restored during a computation.
Stacks also obviate the need for an explicit definition of
transactions
; in
[
2
]
these are sequences of method invocations, which can be easily rendered in the current setting as stacks made up by the sequential composition of commands corresponding to such calls.

The operational semantics relies on judgements of the form
\left<Q,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>\rightarrow\left<Q^{\prime},\text{env}_{T},\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime}\right>
, which in turn are based on judgements of the form
\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v
for evaluating expressions.
The precise rules are in Appendix
A
and they constitute the real operational semantics of the language.
However, to define the semantics of types, we shall need a
typed operational semantics
, which performs some runtime type related checks.
For space reasons, we omit the untyped semantics (which is essentially obtained from the typed one by removing all type related checks) and postpone the definition of the typed one to Section
3.1
, after we have described the language of types.

2.2.
Types and Type Environments for Information Flow Control and Non-Interference

We assume a finite lattice
(\mathcal{S},\sqsubseteq)
of security levels
s
, with
s_{\top}
as the highest level and
s_{\bot}
as the lowest level.
For ease of notation, we shall use the following convention when comparing multiple levels against each other using the ordering relation
\sqsubseteq
:

s\sqsubseteq s_{1},\ldots,s_{h}\triangleq s\sqsubseteq s_{1}\land\ldots\land s\sqsubseteq s_{h}\qquad\qquad s_{1},\ldots,s_{h}\sqsubseteq s_{1}^{\prime},\ldots s_{h}^{\prime}\triangleq s_{1}\sqsubseteq s_{1}^{\prime}\land\ldots\land s_{h}\sqsubseteq s_{h}^{\prime}

The crucial property related to this framework is
information flow control
, stipulating that information at level
s
cannot affect information at levels
strictly below
, or incomparable to,
s
(w.r.t.
\sqsubseteq
). This, in turn, can be used to obtain
non-interference
: if we run a piece of code in memories differing only for information at level
s
or higher, the resulting memories still differ only for information of level
s
or higher.
As usual, the security levels can be used to express both secrecy and integrity. In the first case, higher levels (w.r.t.
\sqsubseteq
) denote secret information, that should not influence lower level (i.e., less secret) information. The second case is dual: higher levels denote untrusted information, that should not influence lower level (i.e., trusted) information.

Our language of types is similar to the one given in
[
2
]
for secure information flows, inspired by the pioneering work from
[
48
]
.

Definition 2.1 (Types and environments).

We define types, base types and typing environments as:

\begin{array}[]{r @{~} c @{~} l @{\qquad} l}T\in\mathcal{T}&::=&B_{s}~~\big|~~~\text{var}(B_{s})~~\big|~~~\text{proc}(\widetilde{B}_{\widetilde{s}})\texttt{:}s\Gamma,\Delta\in\mathcal{E}&::=&\mathcal{N}\rightharpoonup\mathcal{T}\cup\mathcal{E}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{r @{~} c @{~} l @{\qquad} l}B\in\mathcal{B}&::=&\text{idf}~~\big|~~~\text{int}~~\big|~~~\text{bool}~~\big|~~~I\Sigma&::=&\mathcal{N}_{I}\rightharpoonup\mathcal{N}_{I}\end{array}

where

\mathcal{N}_{I}
is a set of interface names, ranged over by
I
, and

\mathcal{N}
, ranged over by
n
, is defined as
\mathcal{N}_{V}\cup\mathcal{N}_{F}\cup\mathcal{N}_{M}\cup\mathcal{N}_{A}\cup\mathcal{N}_{I}
.

For the base types (
\mathcal{B}
),
idf
is the type assigned to method names contained in the magic variable
id
and interfaces are associated to contract addresses. The type of an address
X
is therefore an interface
I
with the signatures of the methods and fields of that contract;
2
2

2

The interface could be extracted from the contract definition itself (as a form of structural typing); however, we prefer to define interfaces separately (thus adopting a nominal typing), to let multiple contracts implement the same interface.

moreover, interfaces are hierarchically organized and we define them as follows:

Definition 2.2 (Interface definition language).

Interface declarations
ID
are given by:

\begin{array}[]{r @{~} c @{~} l @{\qquad} l}ID&::=&\epsilon~~\big|~~~\texttt{interface $I_{1}$ : $I_{2}$ \lx@text@lbrace $IF$ $IM$ \lx@text@rbrace}\ ID\\
IF&::=&\epsilon~~\big|~~~p:\text{var}(B_{s}),IF\qquad\qquad IM::=\epsilon~~\big|~~~f:\text{proc}(\widetilde{B}_{\widetilde{s}})\texttt{:}s,IM\end{array}

where
I_{1}
is the name of the interface and
I_{2}
is the name of its parent in the hierarchy.

The intended meaning of the types
T
is as follows.
The type
B_{s}
(interchangeably written as
(B,s)
) is given to expressions
e
: it denotes that the value of
e
is of type
B
and all values read to evaluate
e
are from containers, i.e. variables and fields, of level
s

or lower
.
The type
\text{var}(B_{s})
is given to containers: it denotes that a field
p
or variable
x
is capable of storing a value of type
B
of level
s

or lower
.
We do not include a type for commands in the language of types; however, we say that a command
S
is safe to execute as
\text{cmd}(s)
if (1) all field assignments made during the execution of
S
are to containers of level
s

or higher
, and (2) all guarded commands (i.e.,
if
and
while
) within
S
must be safe to execute at the same level as their guards (or higher), and this level must be equal to, or higher than,
s
.
3
3

3

As usual, this restriction is necessary to ward against indirect information flows, since a guarded statement could produce observable effects that are indirectly influenced by the expression guard
e
.

Finally, the type
\text{proc}(\widetilde{B}_{\widetilde{s}})\texttt{:}s
is given to methods
f
: it denotes that the body of
f
is safe to execute as
\text{cmd}(s)
, given that the formal arguments have types
\text{var}(\widetilde{B}_{\widetilde{s}})
(that can be alternatively written as
\text{var}(\widetilde{(B,s)})
), which denotes a sequence of types
\text{var}(B_{1},s_{1}),\ldots,\text{var}(B_{h},s_{h})
.

We have three kinds of type environments:

•

\Sigma
records the interface hierarchy:
if we have the declaration
interface
I_{1}
:
I_{2}
{IF IM}
, then
\Sigma(I_{1})=I_{2}
.
For the sake of simplicity, we shall assume that members common to both interfaces also appear in both the parent and the child; i.e. members are not automatically inherited from the parent.
We also assume that all contracts implement only a single interface.

•

\Gamma
records the statically declared interface definitions and statically assigned types of contracts.
Thus, if a contract
X
implements an interface
I
, we have that
\Gamma(X)=I_{s}
, for some
s
, and
\Gamma(I)=\Gamma_{I}
, where
\Gamma_{I}=IF,IM
(and these correspond to the fields and methods declared in
X
).

•

\Delta
records the types of local variables.
We record these in a separate environment, since local variables are allocated/deallocated at runtime and, consequently,
this environment may change during the evolution of the program (whereas the other environments are static).

Given the intended usage of
\Gamma
, only addresses
X
and interface names
I
should appear in its domain.
We express this requirement with the following well-formedness criterion and only consider well-formed environments in what follows.

Definition 2.3 (Well-formedness of
\Gamma
).

A type environment
\Gamma
is
well-formed
if the following holds:

•

Each contract name
X
in
\dom\left(\Gamma\right)
has an interface type
I
; i.e.
\Gamma(X)=I_{s}
and
I\in\dom\left(\Gamma\right)
.

•

Each interface name
I
in
\dom\left(\Gamma\right)
has a type environment entry
\Gamma_{I}
; i.e.
\Gamma(I)=\Gamma_{I}
.

•

No name in
\mathcal{N}_{V}
,
\mathcal{N}_{F}
or
\mathcal{N}_{M}
has an entry in
\Gamma
.

We remark that we only allow single inheritance among interfaces, and we shall therefore assume the existence of an interface
I^{\texttt{top}}
, corresponding to the basic implementation of a contract, which acts as the single root of the inheritance tree.
It has the following definition:

⬇

1

interface

I^{\texttt{top}}

:

I^{\texttt{top}}

{

2

balance

:

\text{var}(\text{int},s_{\top})

3

send

:

\text{proc}()\texttt{:}s_{\bot}

4

fallback

:

\text{proc}()\texttt{:}s_{\bot}

5

}

Note that this definition specifies
I^{\texttt{top}}
as its own parent; since
I^{\texttt{top}}
is the root of the inheritance tree, it alone can inherit from nothing besides itself.
Indeed, although self-inheritance is permitted by the syntax, a simple consistency check on
\Sigma
and
\Gamma
prevents self-inheritance in any cases other than
I^{\texttt{top}}
(see Appendix
B
).
It also ensures that all interface members in a parent interface indeed are supertypes of the corresponding members in their children, where
the key rules in the definition of the subtyping relation
\mathrel{<:}
are the following:

[
sub-field
]

\dfrac{\Sigma\vdash B_{1}\mathrel{<:}B_{2}\qquad s_{1}\sqsubseteq s_{2}}{\Sigma\vdash\text{var}(B_{1},s_{1})\mathrel{<:}\text{var}(B_{2},s_{2})}

[
sub-proc
]

\dfrac{\Sigma\vdash\widetilde{B_{2}}\mathrel{<:}\widetilde{B_{1}}\qquad s_{2}\sqsubseteq s_{1}\qquad\widetilde{s_{2}}\sqsubseteq\widetilde{s_{1}}}{\Sigma\vdash\text{proc}(\widetilde{B_{1},s_{1}})\texttt{:}s_{1}\mathrel{<:}\text{proc}(\widetilde{B_{2},s_{2}})\texttt{:}s_{2}}

These rules stipulate that reads are covariant (all fields are read-only outside of the contract in which they are declared), whereas writes are contravariant (indeed, methods are comparable to ‘write-only’ fields).
Since we use static inheritance, and do not allow dynamic creation of new interfaces, these checks only need to be performed once, when
\Sigma
and
\Gamma
are built.
In the remainder of this paper, we shall therefore assume that any
\Sigma
and
\Gamma
we consider are consistent in this sense.

Finally, for integer and boolean values, the base type can be determined directly from observing the value itself.
However, as the type of a value has both a
data
aspect and a
security
aspect, we also need to assign a security level:
we choose the lowest possible level,
s_{\bot}
, for integers and booleans, since they have no inherent security level attached, and it is always safe to treat a piece of data as having a higher security level than it actually does.
With addresses
X\in\mathcal{N}_{A}
,
we have to retrieve the base type associated to
X
from the given typing environment
\Gamma
, since addresses are
declared
to have a type and a security level.
This is done by the following partial function:

Definition 2.4 (
TypeOf
).

We define the partial function
\text{{TypeOf}}_{\Gamma}(v)
from values to types as:

\text{{TypeOf}}_{\Gamma}(v)=\begin{cases}(\text{idf},s_{\bot})&\text{if $v\in\mathcal{N}_{M}$}\\
(\text{int},s_{\bot})&\text{if $v\in\mathbb{Z}$}\\
(\text{bool},s_{\bot})&\text{if $v\in\mathbb{B}$}\\
\Gamma(v)&\text{if $v\in\mathcal{N}_{A}$ and $v\in\dom\left(\Gamma\right)$}\\
\text{undefined}&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

3.
Semantic Typing for
TinySol

The key steps in our approach are the following:
(1) define runtime type safety in terms of a typed operational semantics;
(2) define typing interpretations as sets of configurations that are runtime type safe (for all steps);
(3) define typing as the union of all typing interpretations;
(4) show that the syntactic type rules of
[
2
]
are compatible with the semantic interpretation.
These steps have to be performed both for expressions and for stacks.

3.1.
Typed operational semantics

We give the semantics of our types in terms of a
typed operational semantics
of
TinySol
, which we create by adding
to the (untyped) operational rules for expressions, statements and stacks (Appendix
A
)
some type related conditions from the static type rules of
[
2
]
(reported in Appendix
C
).
For expressions, judgements take the form

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{B_{s}}\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v
and
should be read as:
e
evaluates to the value
v
of type
B_{s}
, or a subtype thereof, given
\text{env}_{SV}
and the type assumptions in
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta
.
The details are instructive but, for space reasons, we are forced to defer them to Appendix
F
.
For stack configurations, a transitions is of the form

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s}\left<Q,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>\rightarrow\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta^{\prime}\vDash_{s^{\prime}}\left<Q^{\prime},\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime}\right>

and it denotes that we execute the element at the top of the stack
Q
at security level
s
, relative to the code, state and variable environments
\text{env}_{TSV}
and type environments
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta
.
As a result of the execution, the state and variable environments may have changed to
\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime}
,
the type environment for local variables may turn into
\Delta^{\prime}
, if a new variable was dynamically allocated or deallocated, and the security level may have been altered from
s
to
s^{\prime}
, if e.g. the transition steps in a context of heightened security.
The rules are given in Figures
2
and
3.1
.
By fixing a security level
s
, the rules ensure that (1) all containers written to within a command are of level
s
or
higher
, and (2) branching, loops and method calls only depend on information that is of a level
lower
than, or equal to, the level of the variables they modify.
There are a few key points worth noting.

[
r-skip
]

\dfrac{\phantom{P}}{\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s}\left<\texttt{skip};Q,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>\rightarrow\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s}\left<Q,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>}\par

[
r-if
]

\dfrac{\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{(\text{bool},s^{\prime})}\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow b\in\mathbb{B}}{\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s}\left<\texttt{if $e$ then $S_{\text{T}}$ else $S_{\text{F}}$};Q,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>\rightarrow\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s^{\prime}}\left<S_{b};s;Q,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>}\;\left(s\sqsubseteq s^{\prime}\right)\par

To keep the presentation simple (in particular, to avoid exception handling), in this paper we let method invocations always succeed, irrespectively of the amount of currency associated with them.

3.2.
Properties of typed transitions

We start by proving a collection of desirable properties of typed transitions, which ensure that the runtime type related checks indeed guarantee safety in accordance with the intended meaning of the types.
One property
is
s
-equivalence
between two memory states, written
\Gamma;\Delta\vdash\text{env}_{SV}^{1}=_{s}\text{env}_{SV}^{2}
, as in the classic work on non-interference by Volpano, Irvine and Smith
[
48
]
.
Informally, it denotes that
\text{env}_{SV}^{1}
and
\text{env}_{SV}^{2}
contain exactly the same values in all memory cells (fields and variables)
of level
s
or lower
, according to their types in
\Gamma
and
\Delta
.
The formal definition is straightforward (see Appendix
D
).
Another property
is
well-typedness
of memory states, written
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash\text{env}_{SV}
, which denotes that all values stored in
\text{env}_{SV}
indeed are of the respective types of the fields/variables according to the type assignments in
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta
.
This definition is also straightforward (see Appendix
C
) and it is similar to the one used in the syntactic type system of
[
2
]
.

Well-typedness alone ensures that all contract addresses occurring in
\text{env}_{SV}
, either as identifiers or values, have a corresponding interface type
I
in
\Gamma
.
However, it does
not
ensure that the address also contains a contract with a corresponding implementation matching
I
.
This property is obviously also desirable to avoid stuck configurations, so we introduce the notion of a
consistency
check, written
\Gamma\vdash\text{env}_{TSV}
,
to ensure it
(see Appendix
E
).

Given a typed configuration
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s}\left<Q,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>
, well-typedness and consistency together ensure that
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta
and
\text{env}_{SV}
are compatible.
We must also impose a similar restriction on stacks
Q
, which ensures that the free variable names, field names and addresses occurring in
Q
are in the domains of
\text{env}_{SV}
.
Furthermore, since a transition can restore a variable environment from the stack
Q
, we must also naturally require that all return symbols
(\text{env}_{V},\Delta)
occurring in
Q
are also well-typed and consistent.
Lastly, if a stack
Q
represents a (partially evaluated) actual program, then all security levels
s_{1},\ldots,s_{h}
in
Q
must occur in
descending
order, i.e.
s_{1}\sqsupseteq\ldots\sqsupseteq s_{h}
, and these must be lower than the current execution level
s
, i.e.
s\sqsupseteq s_{1}
.
We ensure these by a
well-formedness
check on stacks, written
\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{SV},s\vdash Q
(see Appendix
G
for a formal definition).
We write
\text{{fst}}(Q)
for the first security level occurring in
Q
, which will also be the highest if
Q
is well-formed.
If no security level occurs in
Q
, we let
\text{{fst}}(Q)=s_{\bot}
.
Together, well-typedness, consistency and well-formedness ensure that the various components of the configuration are not simply chosen randomly, but in fact constitute an executable program.
We can now formulate a first property of the typed operational semantics, i.e. that the coercion property holds for stacks (the proof is in Appendix
H
).

Proposition 3.1 (Coercion property for stacks).

Assume
Q
satisfies that all security levels in
Q
occur in descending order.
For all type environments
\Sigma
,
\Gamma
and
\Delta
, security levels
s_{1}\sqsupseteq s_{2}\sqsupseteq\text{{fst}}(Q)
, and environments
\text{env}_{TSV}
, it holds that, for every
s_{1}^{\prime}
such that
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s_{1}}\left<Q,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>\rightarrow\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta^{\prime}\vDash_{s_{1}^{\prime}}\left<Q^{\prime},\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime}\right>
, there exists an
s_{2}^{\prime}
such that
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s_{2}}\left<Q,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>\rightarrow\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta^{\prime}\vDash_{s_{2}^{\prime}}\left<Q^{\prime},\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime}\right>
.

Note that Proposition
3.1
does not directly relate the two resulting security levels
s_{1}^{\prime}
and
s_{2}^{\prime}
.
Indeed, several of the rules allow a higher security level to be chosen; so, depending on the structure of the security lattice, different choices could be made in the two transitions.
However, if we assume that the same choice is made in both executions (e.g. the highest, or lowest, possible security level for which the transition can be concluded), then we would also have that
s_{1}^{\prime}\sqsupseteq s_{2}^{\prime}
.

We can also show a result that allows us to abstract away from the specific values stored in the state environment
\text{env}_{SV}
.
Indeed, given some
\Sigma
,
\Gamma
and
\Delta
, we can construct environments
\text{env}_{SV}
such that well-typedness and consistency are ensured to hold,
as the following definition and lemma show.

Definition 3.2 (Construction of
\text{env}_{SV}
).

Given a
\Sigma
,
\Gamma
and
\Delta
, let
\mathcal{D}\triangleq\dom\left(\Gamma\right)\cap\mathcal{N}_{A}
denote the set of
declared
address names in
\Gamma
.

•

We construct
\text{env}_{V}
as a list of pairs
x_{1}:v_{1},\ldots,x_{h}:v_{h}
such that
\left\{\,x_{1},\ldots,x_{h}\,\right\}=\dom\left(\Delta\right)
and each
v_{i}
is such that, if
\Delta(x_{i})=\text{var}(B_{2},s_{2})
, then
\text{{TypeOf}}_{\Gamma}(v_{i})=(B_{1},s_{1})
for
\Sigma\vdash B_{1}\mathrel{<:}B_{2}
and
s_{1}\sqsubseteq s_{2}
, and
v_{i}\in\mathcal{D}
, whenever
v_{i}\in\mathcal{N}_{A}
.

•

We construct
\text{env}_{S}
as a list of pairs
X_{1}:\text{env}_{F}^{1},\ldots,X_{h}:\text{env}_{F}^{h}
such that
\left\{\,X_{1},\ldots,X_{h}\,\right\}=\mathcal{D}
and each
\text{env}_{F}^{i}
is constructed as follows:
Assume that
\Gamma(X_{i})=I_{s}
and
\Gamma(I)=\Gamma_{F}^{I},\Gamma_{M}^{I}
, then,
\text{env}_{F}^{i}
is a list of pairs
p_{1}:v_{1},\ldots,p_{h}:v_{h}
such that
\left\{\,p_{1},\ldots,p_{h}\,\right\}=\dom\left(\Gamma_{F}^{I}\right)
and each
v_{i}
is such that, if
\Gamma_{F}^{I}(x_{i})=\text{var}(B_{2},s_{2})
, then
\text{{TypeOf}}_{\Gamma}(v_{i})=(B_{1},s_{1})
for
\Sigma\vdash B_{1}\mathrel{<:}B_{2}
and
s_{1}\sqsubseteq s_{2}
, and
v_{i}\in\mathcal{D}
, whenever
v_{i}\in\mathcal{N}_{A}
.

Lemma 3.3 (Correctness of the construction).

Given a
\Sigma
,
\Gamma
and
\Delta
, if
\text{env}_{SV}
is built according to Definition
3.2
, then

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash\text{env}_{SV}
(well-typedness),
and
\Gamma\vdash\text{env}_{SV}
(consistency).

Corollary 3.4 (Safe state abstraction).

For all environments
\Sigma
,
\Gamma
,
\Delta
and
\text{env}_{T}
, security level
s
, and
\text{env}_{SV}^{1},\text{env}_{SV}^{2}
built according to Definition
3.2
, it holds that, for every
s_{1}^{\prime}
such that
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s}\left<Q,\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{1}\right>\rightarrow\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\vDash_{s_{1}^{\prime}}\left<Q^{\prime},\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{1^{\prime}}\right>
, there exists an
s_{2}^{\prime}
such that
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s}\left<Q,\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{2}\right>\rightarrow\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta_{2}^{\prime}\vDash_{s_{2}^{\prime}}\left<Q^{\prime},\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{2^{\prime}}\right>
.

We can now ascertain that the typed semantics does ensure the intended notion of type safety; the following two theorems are indeed the first cornerstone results of our approach.

Theorem 3.5 (Runtime preservation).

Assume that

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash\text{env}_{SV}
(Well-typedness),

\Gamma\vdash\text{env}_{TSV}
(Consistency), and

\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{SV};s\vdash Q
(Well-formedness).
If
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s}\left<Q,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>\rightarrow\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta^{\prime}\vDash_{s^{\prime}}\left<Q^{\prime},\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime}\right>
,
then

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta^{\prime}\vdash\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime}
(Well-typedness),

\Gamma\vdash\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime}
(Consistency),

\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime};s^{\prime}\vdash Q^{\prime}
(Well-formedness), and
for all
s^{\prime\prime}
such that
s\not\sqsubseteq s^{\prime\prime}
it holds that
\Gamma\vdash\text{env}_{S}=_{s^{\prime\prime}}\text{env}_{S}^{\prime}
(
s^{\prime\prime}
-equivalence).

Theorem
3.5
assures us that the properties of well-typedness, consistency, and well-formedness are preserved by the typed semantics; additionally, any change to
\text{env}_{S}
must be to a field that is of level
s
or higher.
The latter is in accordance with the meaning of the static type judgment
\text{cmd}(s)
, corresponding to the parameterisation of the turnstile
\vDash_{s}
in the typed semantics.
All variables of a level
strictly below
, or incomparable to,
s
will be unaffected by the transition steps; so, the environments
\text{env}_{S}
and
\text{env}_{S}^{\prime}
agree on all such entries.
This is the safety property induced by the types, since executing the stack at level
s
means that no variables of a level
lower
than, or incomparable to,
s
may be modified (cf. rules
,
and
),
and information is not permitted to flow from a higher level to a lower one.
Indeed, we can also use Theorem
3.5
to show that the typed semantics ensures non-interference for stack executions.

Theorem 3.6 (Runtime non-interference for stacks).

Assume that

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash\text{env}_{SV}^{1}
and
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash\text{env}_{SV}^{2}
(Well-typedness),

\Gamma\vdash\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{1}
and
\Gamma\vdash\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{2}
(Consistency),

\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{SV}^{1},s\vdash Q
and
\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{SV}^{2},s\vdash Q
(Well-formedness), and

\Gamma;\Delta\vdash\text{env}_{SV}^{1}=_{s}\text{env}_{SV}^{2}
(
s
-equivalence).
If
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s}\left<Q,\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{i}\right>\rightarrow\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta^{\prime}\vDash_{s^{\prime}}\left<Q_{i},\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{i^{\prime}}\right>
, for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
then
\Gamma;\Delta^{\prime}\vdash\text{env}_{SV}^{1^{\prime}}=_{s}\text{env}_{SV}^{2^{\prime}}
.

It is worth noting that the executions of the configurations
\left<Q,\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{1}\right>
and
\left<Q,\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{2}\right>
actually allow for two different methods to be called.
Suppose for example that
Q
is of the form
call
x
.
f
(
\widetilde{e}
)$
e_{2}
, and
x
is a variable of type
\text{var}(I,s^{\prime})
for some level
s^{\prime}
strictly higher than
s
.
This would allow it to contain two different addresses, e.g.
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
in the two executions, which would lead to two different methods being called, albeit with the same signature.
Well-typedness and consistency of the two environments ensure that either both methods exist or neither does, because both
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
must be addresses of contracts that implement all the methods of the interface
I
.
Thus, the two resulting variable environments,
\text{env}_{V}^{1^{\prime}}
and
\text{env}_{V}^{2^{\prime}}
, will still contain exactly the same variable entries, and with values agreeing at all levels not higher than
s
.

On the other hand, calling two different methods would also mean that the two different
balance
fields of the two callees would be different in
\text{env}_{S}^{1^{\prime}}
and
\text{env}_{S}^{2^{\prime}}
(e.g., in
\text{env}_{S}^{1^{\prime}}
it might be
X_{1}.\texttt{balance}
that was modified, but
X_{2}.\texttt{balance}
in
\text{env}_{S}^{2^{\prime}}
).
However, this does not contradict
Theorem
3.6
because of the side condition of the rules
and
, which are the only two rules that could have been used to conclude such a transition (rule
cannot have been used, since it does not modify
\text{env}_{S}
):
this side condition states that
s^{\prime}\sqsubseteq s_{3},s_{4}
, where
s_{3}
and
s_{4}
are the levels of the
balance
fields of the caller and callee, respectively.
As we assumed that
s^{\prime}
is strictly greater than
s
, then so are both
s_{3}
and
s_{4}
, which means that this difference will not affect the
s
-equality of the two environments.

3.3.
Typing interpretations and semantic type rules for stacks and commands

Theorem
3.5
ensures that a configuration
\left<Q,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>
does not get stuck due to a malformed stack or an attempt to access a contract member on an address that does not contain an implementation.
Once we have ruled out such ‘trivial’ type errors, what remains is that a stuck configuration can only derive from three situations:
(1)
Q
is of the form
\bot
, meaning we have reached the bottom of the stack, so the execution terminates normally;
(2)
Q
is of the form
\texttt{throw};Q^{\prime}
, meaning an exception was thrown, so the execution terminates abnormally; and
(3) the corresponding untyped transition would cause a violation of
type safety
by allowing an insecure information flow.
Of these, the first two forms represent terminal stacks, defined as

\mathcal{TQ}\triangleq\{\bot\}\cup\{\texttt{throw};Q\ |\ Q\in\mathcal{Q}\}
;
they are obviously safe to execute, since they have no transitions, and can be detected simply by examining the structure of
Q
.
Thus, the key point to note here is that
a non-terminal stuck configuration indicates a runtime type error
.
We shall use this idea to formulate a notion of
typing interpretations
for stacks
Q
, in such a way that
Q
must either be one of the two first forms, or have a typed transition (with some appropriately shaped
\text{env}_{SV}
) such that the reduct is again contained in the typing interpretation.

However, it is important to note that the type constraints represented by
\Delta
and the current security level
s
can
change
after a transition step.
Hence, also
\Delta
and
s
must be included in the typing interpretation, together with
Q
.
Our notion of typing interpretations will therefore not be for stacks
Q
alone, but for
stack type triplets

(Q,\Delta,s)
, where
\Delta
and
s
represent the currently active variable type environment and security level under which
Q
is executing.
To make this clearer, we shall first define a new transition system directly on the triplets themselves.

Definition 3.7 (Type lifted transition).

Assume
\Sigma
,
\Gamma
and
\text{env}_{T}
are fixed.
A
type lifted transition system
is a tuple
(\mathcal{P},\Mapsto)
, where
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{E}\times\mathcal{S}
is the set of stack type triplets
(Q,\Delta,s)
and the transition relation
\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{T}\vDash(Q,\Delta,s)\Mapsto(Q^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime},s^{\prime})
holds whenever there exist an
\text{env}_{SV}
built from
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta
according to Definition
3.2
and an
\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime}
such that
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash_{s}\left<Q,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>\rightarrow\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta^{\prime};\text{env}_{T}\vDash_{s^{\prime}}\left<Q^{\prime},\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime}\right>
.

By Corollary
3.4
, we know that, if there exists an
\text{env}_{SV}^{1}
such that the configuration
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s}\left<Q,\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{1}\right>
has a transition, then a transition will also exist for all similarly shaped (according to Definition
3.2
)
\text{env}_{SV}^{2}
.
All such
\text{env}_{SV}
are ensured to be well-typed, consistent and compatible with
(Q,\Delta,s)
by construction, and one way to view this quantification over all such environments is to think of it as a quantification over all possible, appropriately shaped and typed
inputs
to the program on the stack.
This is quite natural, since we obviously want a notion of safety that does not depend on the
actual
values being passed to the program, but only on the
types
of those values.
It is in this sense that the transition system is
type lifted:
the shape of the transition system is not determined by actual values, but by the
types
of those values.

Different inputs may of course yield different reducts, e.g. in case the stack
Q
had an
if-else
statement at the top, and the environments yield different values for the guard expression.
Hence, there may also be more than one possible reduct for a given triplet
P
in the type lifted transition system, and each of them must be contained in the typing interpretation.
We define it as follows:

Definition 3.8 (Typing interpretations).

A
typing interpretation
for stack type triplets w.r.t. environments
\Sigma
,
\Gamma
and
\text{env}_{T}
, written
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
, is a set of stack type triplets
P=(Q,\Delta,s)
such that, for every
(Q,\Delta,s)\in\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
, it holds that

(1)

Q\in\mathcal{TQ}
, or

(2)

\exists P_{1}^{\prime}
such that
\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{T}\vDash(Q,\Delta,s)\Mapsto P_{1}^{\prime}
and

\forall P_{2}^{\prime}
such that
\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{T}\vDash(Q,\Delta,s)\Mapsto P_{2}^{\prime}
it holds that
P_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
.

The
typing
of stack type triplets w.r.t.
\Sigma
,
\Gamma
and
\text{env}_{T}
, written
\nu\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
, is the union of all typing interpretations
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
.
We write
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash Q:\text{cmd}(s)
if there exists a typing interpretation
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
containing
(Q,\Delta,s)
.
Likewise, we write
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash S:\text{cmd}(s)
if there exists a typing interpretation
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
containing
(S;\bot,\Delta,s)
.

Notice that the coinductive nature of typing interpretations allows for the possibility of divergent executions.
Moreover, the existence of a typing interpretation
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
containing
(Q,\Delta,s)
expresses that
Q
is safe to execute at level
s
or lower (down to
\text{{fst}}(Q)
) for all steps, according to the type constraints in
\Sigma
,
\Gamma
and
\Delta
.
Note also that typing interpretations for stack type triplets are formulated relative to a
particular
choice of code environment
\text{env}_{T}
.
This is necessary because of the presence of fallback functions, which can place (statically) untypable code on the stack.
All we therefore can require is that such code must be runtime safe, if it is executed, which is what the existence of a typing interpretation expresses.

By using the type-lifted transition system in Definition
3.8
, we abstract away from any particular choice of
\text{env}_{SV}
:
we only require that one can be built from
\Sigma
and
\Gamma
, and from the
\Delta
in the stack type triplet.
In the definition, we discard the environments
\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime}
from the configuration in the reduct of the underlying typed transition, since we use the type-lifted transition system, but we require that the triplet
P_{2}^{\prime}=(Q_{2}^{\prime},\Delta_{2}^{\prime},s_{2}^{\prime})
must again be in the typing interpretation.
This works because, by Theorem
3.5
, we know that the properties of well-typedness, consistency and well-formedness are preserved by the typed semantics, so this
\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime}
will again be amongst those that can be built from
\Sigma
,
\Gamma
and
\Delta_{2}^{\prime}
in the next step.

Using Definition
3.8
, we can now show some properties of typing interpretations for stacks; all the proofs are in Appendix
I
.
The first one can be used as a proof technique for showing that a stack cannot be typed under the prescriptions of the given environments.

Lemma 3.9.

If
\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{T}\vDash(Q,\Delta,s)\Mapsto^{*}(Q^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime},s^{\prime})\not\Mapsto
, for some
\Delta^{\prime}
,
s^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}\not\in\mathcal{TQ}
, then
(Q,\Delta,s)\not\in\nu\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
.

We can now also prove that typing is closed under various operations, including, importantly, the syntactic constructors of the language, given certain assumptions.
As an example of what is to come, we can now show that typing is closed under coercions.

Lemma 3.10 (Typing coercion for stacks).

If
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash Q:\text{cmd}(s_{1})
and

s_{1}\sqsupseteq s_{2}\sqsupseteq\text{{fst}}(Q)
, then
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash Q:\text{cmd}(s_{2})
.

Corollary 3.11 (Typing coercion for statements).

If
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash S:\text{cmd}(s_{1})
and
s_{1}\sqsupseteq s_{2}
, then
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash S:\text{cmd}(s_{2})
.

The previous results are instructive for two reasons.
Firstly, they are proved by coinduction: we construct a new candidate typing interpretation
\mathcal{R}
from an existing one, and show that
\mathcal{R}
is still a typing interpretation (that is contained in the typing of
\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{T}
, since
\nu\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
is the union of all typing interpretations).
Secondly, both results have a particular form, with a number of premises involving typing that imply a single conclusion, still involving typing; hence, they can alternatively be written in the form of
inference rules
(in particular, Corollary
3.11
resembles the contravariant coercion rule for typing statements in
[
2
]
).
However, unlike the typing rules from
[
2
]
, these rules have been
proved admissible
based on the definition of typing, rather than being defined a priori; this is one of the key differences between the syntactic and the semantic approaches.

We shall now state a collection of compatibility lemmas for each of the stack operations; the lemmas are syntax directed, following the syntax of stacks. Indeed, they can again be written as inference rules; these can be found, together with their proofs, in Appendix
I
.
As with the coercion rules, these rules closely resemble the static type rules for stacks (see Figure
in Appendix
C
).

Lemma 3.12 (Bottom).

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash\bot:\text{cmd}(s)
.

Lemma 3.13 (Statement).

If
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash S:\text{cmd}(s)
and
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash Q:\text{cmd}(s)
,
then
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash S;Q:\text{cmd}(s)
.

Lemma 3.14 (End of scope).

If
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash Q:\text{cmd}(s)
,
then
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta,x:\text{var}(B^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}});\text{env}_{T}\vDash\text{del}(x);Q:\text{cmd}(s)
.

Lemma 3.15 (Method return).

If
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta^{\prime};\text{env}_{T}\vDash Q:\text{cmd}(s)
and
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta^{\prime}\vdash\text{env}_{V}^{\prime}
,
then
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash(\text{env}_{V}^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime});Q:\text{cmd}(s)
.

Lemma 3.16 (Security level restore).

If
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash Q:\text{cmd}(s^{\prime})
and
s\sqsupseteq s^{\prime}
, then
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash s^{\prime};Q:\text{cmd}(s)
.

Similarly,
to handle the syntactic category of statements, we shall now provide lemmas for type safety assertions of the form
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash S:\text{cmd}(s)
, whose proofs are again in Appendix
I
.

Lemma 3.17 (Skip).

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash\texttt{skip}:\text{cmd}(s)
.

Lemma 3.18 (Throw).

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash\texttt{throw}:\text{cmd}(s)
.

Lemma 3.19 (Sequential composition).

If
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash S_{1}:\text{cmd}(s)
and
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash S_{2}:\text{cmd}(s)
, then
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash S_{1};S_{2}:\text{cmd}(s)
.

Lemma 3.20 (If-then-else).

If
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash e:\text{bool}_{s}
,

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash S_{\text{T}}:\text{cmd}(s)
and

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash S_{\text{F}}:\text{cmd}(s)
, then
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash\texttt{if $e$ then $S_{\text{T}}$ else $S_{\text{F}}$}:\text{cmd}(s)
.

Lemma 3.21 (While).

If
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash e:\text{bool}_{s}
and
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\text{env}_{T}\vDash S:\text{cmd}(s)
,
then
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash\texttt{while $e$ do $S$}:\text{cmd}(s)
.

Lemma 3.22 (Variable declaration).

If
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash e:B_{s_{1}}
and
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta,x:\text{var}(B_{s_{1}});\text{env}_{T}\vDash S:\text{cmd}(s)
,
then
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash\texttt{$\text{var}(B_{s_{1}})$ $x$ := $e$ in $S$}:\text{cmd}(s)
.

Lemma 3.23 (Variable assignment).

If
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash e:B_{s}
and
\Delta(x)=\text{var}(B_{s})
, then
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash\texttt{$x$ := $e$}:\text{cmd}(s)
.

Lemma 3.24 (Field assignment).

If
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash e:B_{s}
,
\Delta(\texttt{this})=\text{var}(I_{s_{1}})
,
\Gamma(I)(p)=\text{var}(B_{s})
, and
s_{1}\sqsubseteq s
, then
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vdash\texttt{this.$p$ := $e$}:\text{cmd}(s)
.

Before proceeding with the final compatibility lemmas for method calls, we shall need to define a semantic notion of type safety for the code environment
\text{env}_{T}
, using Definition
3.8
.
This is written
\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{T}\vDash\text{env}_{T}
and states that there exists a typing interpretation for each method body
S
, with
\Delta
and
s
derived from the method signature in
\Gamma
.
Including the premise
\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{T}\vDash\text{env}_{T}
in the following lemmas requires that every method declaration in
\text{env}_{T}
(except fallback functions) must be semantically type safe.
This is of course a reasonable requirement, since a standard Subject-Reduction theorem also would require well-typedness of all the environments.
However, it is worth pointing out that this is an over-approximation, since we actually only need the method
f
(and its possible subtypes) declared on the interface
I^{Y}
(and its possible subtypes) to be type safe, in order for the proof to work.
This restriction can thus be weakened without affecting the proof.
We preferred to use the more general requirement
\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{T}\vDash\text{env}_{T}
merely to keep the premises of the lemmas simpler.

Lemma 3.25 (Method call).

Assume that
\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{T}\vDash\text{env}_{T}
,

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash e_{1}:I^{Y}_{s}
,

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash e_{2}:\text{int}_{s}
,

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash e_{i}^{\prime}:(B_{i}^{\prime},s_{i}^{\prime})
for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,h\}
,

\Delta(\texttt{this})=I^{X}_{s_{1}}
,

\Gamma(I^{X})(\texttt{balance})=\text{var}(\text{int}_{s_{3}})
,

\Gamma(I^{Y})(\texttt{balance})=\text{var}(\text{int}_{s_{4}})
,

\Gamma(I^{Y})(f)=\text{proc}((B_{1}^{\prime},s_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(B_{h}^{\prime},s_{h}^{\prime}))\texttt{:}s
, and

s_{1}\sqsubseteq s\sqsubseteq s_{3},s_{4}
.
Then
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash\texttt{call $e_{1}$.$f$($e_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,e_{h}^{\prime}$)\$$e_{2}$}:\text{cmd}(s)
.

Lemma 3.26 (Delegate call).

Assume that
\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{T}\vDash\text{env}_{T}
,

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash e:I^{Y}_{s}
,

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash e_{i}^{\prime}:(B_{i}^{\prime},s_{i}^{\prime})
for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,h\}
,

\Delta(\texttt{this})=I^{X}_{s_{1}}
,

\Sigma\vdash I^{X}\mathrel{<:}I^{Y}
,

\forall q\in(\dom\left(\Gamma(I^{Y})\right)\cap(\mathcal{N}_{F}\cup\{\texttt{balance}\})).\ \Gamma(I^{Y})(q)=\Gamma(I^{X})(q)
,

\Gamma(I^{Y})(f)=\text{proc}((B_{1}^{\prime},s_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(B_{h}^{\prime},s_{h}^{\prime}))\texttt{:}s
, and

s_{1}\sqsubseteq s
.
Then
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vdash\texttt{dcall $e$.$f$($e_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,e_{h}^{\prime}$)}:\text{cmd}(s)
.

The similarity between the syntactic typing rules from
[
2
]
and the corresponding semantic ones associated to Lemma
3.17
–
3.26
allows us to relate the two judgements; this is the second cornerstone result of our approach.

Theorem 3.27 (Compatibility for statements).

If
\Sigma;\Gamma;\emptyset\vdash\text{env}_{T}
and
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash S:\text{cmd}(s)
, then
\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{T}\vDash\text{env}_{T}
and
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash S:\text{cmd}(s)
.

Notice that, also for the semantic typing, the rules corresponding to the aforementioned lemmas provide only an
approximation
to the set of type safe programs, just as the syntactic type rules only define well-typedness as an approximation to type safety.
Both must necessarily induce some
slack
, that is, statements
S
for which safety cannot be concluded by the rules, but which nevertheless behave in a type safe way at runtime (i.e. whose typed transitions would not be stuck).
However, the important thing to note here is that using the semantic type rules is
optional
:
there is always the possibility of showing safety for a statement
S
directly by giving an explicit typing interpretation instead, to satisfy a premise anywhere in the system.
A syntactic type system would leave us no way to reason about such statements, e.g. to show that a compound statement
S^{\prime}
is safe, even if it contains
S
as a sub-statement.
The semantic type rules now give us that option.
In particular, it now becomes possible to approach safety for some usages of the fallback function.

3.4.
Safe and unsafe fallback calls

Armed with our notion of type safety, we can now return to the problem of how to ensure safety of the fallback function.
We give a semantic typing rule for fallback function calls in Figure
4
; it can of course also be stated as a lemma (with an associated proof) but we shall omit this, since the steps are the same as in the proof of Lemma
3.25
.
The only difference is that we obtain the body
S
of the fallback function and the type environment
\Delta^{\prime}
from an explicit quantification over addresses
Y
in the side condition, rather than having the more general statement
\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{T}\vDash\text{env}_{T}
in the premise.

[
st-fcall
]

\dfrac{\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash e_{1}:I^{Y}_{s}\quad\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash e_{2}:\text{int}_{s}\quad\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash\widetilde{e}:\widetilde{B}_{\widetilde{s}}}{\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash\texttt{call $e_{1}$.$f$($\widetilde{e}$)\$$e_{2}$}:\text{cmd}(s)}\;\left(s_{1}\sqsubseteq s\sqsubseteq s_{3},s_{4}\right)

where:

~\hskip-19.91684pt\begin{array}[]{ll}f\notin\dom\left(\Gamma(I^{Y})\right)&\Delta(\texttt{this})=\text{var}(I^{X}_{s_{1}})\\
\Gamma(I^{X})(\texttt{balance})=\text{var}(\text{int}_{s_{3}})&\Gamma(I^{Y})(\texttt{balance})=\text{var}(\text{int}_{s_{4}})\end{array}

\forall Y

~\in\dom\left(\text{env}_{T}\right)\;.\;\Gamma(Y)=I^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}\land\Sigma\vdash I^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}\mathrel{<:}I^{Y}_{s}\implies\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta^{\prime};\text{env}_{T}\vDash S:\text{cmd}(s)

where:

~{\begin{array}[]{l}\text{env}_{T}(Y)(\texttt{fallback})=(\epsilon,S)\\
\Delta^{\prime}=\texttt{this}:\text{var}(I^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}),\texttt{value}:\text{var}(\text{int}_{s}),\texttt{sender}:\text{var}(I^{\texttt{top}}_{s_{\top}}),\texttt{id}:\text{var}(\text{idf}_{s}),\texttt{args}:\text{var}(\widetilde{B}_{\widetilde{s}})\end{array}}

Figure 4.
The semantic type rule for fallback function calls.

That difference is unimportant in
proving
admissibility of the rule, but it does make a difference in
using
it for type checking a piece of code.
In Lemmas
3.25
and
3.26
, the statement
\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{T}\vDash\text{env}_{T}
is independent of the other premises;
it does not have to be proved for each application of one of the aforementioned rules.
In contrast, in the rule
, the quantification is over all addresses
Y
satisfying a condition that depends on the type of the expression
e_{1}
. This, in turn, determines the type environment
\Delta^{\prime}
and the level
s
for which the body
S
must be type safe.
The truth value of this side condition may thus have to be evaluated anew
each time
the rule is applied, whereas
\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{T}\vDash\text{env}_{T}
can be shown separately, once and for all.
In essence, the rule requires that the fallback functions of
every
contract implementing the interface
I^{Y}
,
or one of its descendants
, must be type safe at level
s
.
This extra step is necessary, since
I^{Y}_{s}
is not guaranteed to be the actual type of
e_{1}
, but only its runtime type.
Any address having an actual type
I^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}
, where
I^{\prime}
is a descendant of
I^{Y}
and
s^{\prime}\sqsubseteq s
, is also in the set of values inhabiting the type
I^{Y}_{s}
; hence they too could be obtained from the evaluation of
e_{1}
, and they must therefore be included as well.

As with the other semantic type rules, rule
only requires that the premises and side conditions must hold, but it imposes no restrictions on how they are shown.
A proof of type safety for a fallback function body
S
, w.r.t. some type environment
\Delta
and security level
s
, could simply be given in the form of an explicit typing interpretation
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
containing
(S;\bot,\Delta,s)
.
Such a typing interpretation is just a set of triplets, and it could therefore in principle be provided by the contract creator and stored together with the code itself, to witness the type safety of the contract’s fallback function.
This would then allow any user of the code, who might
generate a method call that will result in an invocation of that fallback function, to use this typing interpretation to satisfy the premise of rule
when type checking his own code; this can be seen as a form of
proof-carrying code

[
33
]
.

Figure 5.
An example of the pointer-to-implementation pattern.

As an example of a safe usage of the fallback function, consider the code in Figure
5
.
The
Proxy
contract uses its fallback function to forward all method calls to an implementation residing elsewhere, in the contract
X
.
This is a typical, practical application of the fallback function construct, which allows the functionality of a smart contract to be updated, despite the immutable nature of the blockchain (see
[
38
]
for an extensive discussion of this and related patterns for upgradability).
The contract provides a single method,
update(x)
, which allows the contract owner (having the unique address
A
) to update the
impl
field, which holds the reference to the implementation.
The functionality can then be updated by deploying a new implementation contract and updating the reference in the proxy.
Any other method call to this contract is forwarded to the implementation through the reference in
impl
.
This is an example of the so called
pointer-to-implementation pattern
.

The implementation contract
X
provides methods
f_{1}
, …,
f_{h}
and a fallback function which only does a
skip
.
Thus, even though
Proxy
uses
id
to generically forward all method calls (except
update
) to
X
, we know that there are only finitely many possible continuations after any method call to
Proxy
:
either
update
, or one of
f_{1}
, …,
f_{h}
, or
skip
(when the fallback function is invoked).
Thus, if none of these methods can cause an insecure information flow, then any
call Proxy.
f
()$
z
for
f\neq\texttt{update}
will be safe too.
This cannot be inferred directly, using the other semantic type rules, because the interface definition for
I^{P}
will not contain a declaration of the method
f
.
However, with an explicit typing interpretation witnessing the safety of the execution of
call this.impl.id()$value
, which will include the executions of
f_{1}
, …,
f_{h}
, the call can nevertheless be shown to be safe by using rule
.
Of course,
finding
such a typing interpretation may be a monumental task, even with only finitely many possible continuations.
We return to this issue below, in Section
4
.

Of course, not all usages of the fallback function are type safe, even with semantic typing.
Suppose, for example, that we replaced the forwarding method call in the fallback function of
Proxy
with a
delegate
call; i.e.

⬇

func

fallback
()

{

dcall

this
.
impl
.
id
(
args
)

}

(
\dagger
)

Any attempt to invoke
Proxy.
f_{i}
()
would now result in
f_{i}
being executed in the context of
Proxy
, rather than
X
, thereby allowing it to mutate the state of
Proxy
.
However, using the typed semantics, this execution would be stuck because of the type constraint on the rule
for delegate calls, which requires that the caller must be a subtype of the callee.
Clearly, it does not hold that
\Sigma\vdash I^{P}\mathrel{<:}I^{X}
, since
X
declares the methods
f_{1},\ldots,f_{h}
whereas
Proxy
does not contain similar declarations; so, this constraint cannot be satisfied.
5
5

5

And conversely, if
Proxy
in fact
did
declare methods
f_{1},\ldots,f_{h}
too, then none of these methods in
X
could be invoked through the fallback function of
Proxy
, since it is only executed on calls to
non-existing
members.

Thus, no typing interpretation can be created to witness the safety of
(\dagger)
, cf. Lemma
3.9
.
Altering our
Proxy
example with
(\dagger)
is in fact a key ingredient for mounting an attack similar to the Parity Multisig Wallet (PMW) attack
[
24
;
28
]
mentioned in the Introduction, that thus is prevented by our approach.
The second ingredient is adding to the implementation contract
X
a method declaration

⬇

func

init
(
x
)

{

this
.
owner

:=

x

}

which modifies a local field in
X
called
owner
.
However, since delegate calls are executed in the context of the
caller
, this allows an attacker to call
Proxy.init(A’)
with his own address
A’
as argument, which then results in the
owner
field of
Proxy
being updated instead.
Thus, the attacker can take over ownership of the
Proxy
contract and drain it of all funds.

Variadic arguments

Another issue pertains to the magic variable
args
, which we have hitherto avoided using.
This variable acts as a variadic argument list, i.e. an array of unknown length with values of mixed types.
This can easily cause configurations to be stuck, due to a wrong number of arguments or mismatched types in method calls, if a method call is issued with
args
in place of an ordinary argument list.
However,
args
can also be encapsulated in a safe manner, although this will require some extensions to the type language;
we now sketch the steps involved.

An example with safe encapsulation of variadic arguments.

As a first step, suppose we admit operators such as
len
,
typeof
and array indexing into the language of expressions, which can be used to extract information from
args
at runtime.
Consider now the code in Figure
6
.
By using these constructs, we can meticulously check the number of arguments present in
args
and their actual types, and then pass control to various handlers accordingly.
Again, we ensure safety by reducing the infinite number of possible values of
args
to yield only a finite number of possible continuations.
One problem here is the operator
typeof
: we have hitherto assumed that all expression operators
op
can be given a type
\widetilde{B}\to B
, but the
typeof
operator must be able to operate on a value of
any
type.
Hence, it is polymorphic, and the language of types for operators must therefore be extended with polymorphic types; we must furthermore extend the language of base types
B
with a type for type objects, e.g.
type
.
Thus, the type of
typeof
must be
\forall\alpha\;.\;\alpha\to\text{type}
, where
\alpha
is a type variable.
These are technical details that are, in a sense, orthogonal to the issue of semantic typing, so we shall not pursue them further here.
However, extending the type language with these features would be a relevant avenue for future work.
