Figure 7.
A proxy contract using both explicit forwarding and fallback.

Finally, combining the preceding examples, we can show how to create a variant of the
Proxy
contract that allows both upgrading and extending the functionality, but avoids the vulnerability of the PMW attack.
Consider the code in Figure
7
: we now let the
Proxy
declare methods
f_{1},\ldots,f_{h}
and then
explicitly
forward each call to the corresponding method in the implementation
X
.
This avoids the problem of allowing an unintended call to a method like
init
, as in the PMW attack, whilst still permitting the method bodies to mutate the state of
Proxy
.
All other method calls are then generically forwarded using the fallback function, but using the direct call style.
These calls thus cannot modify the state of
Proxy
, but they can still extend its functionality.

Because of the type restriction on the rule
,
I^{P}
must be a subtype of
I^{X}
, hence
X
cannot declare any methods or fields that are not also declared in
Proxy
.
All generically forwarded calls, which necessarily cannot be to any of
f_{1},\ldots,f_{h}
, will therefore invoke the fallback function of
X
.
It can then use the aforementioned
len
and
typeof
operators to ensure that the argument list has the correct number of elements and types, as in the preceding example, before forwarding these calls to another contract
Y
(not shown) containing the extension methods
g_{1},g_{2},\ldots
.
This extra level of indirection allows both the implementation in
X
and the extensions in
Y
to be upgraded.

Note that using explicit forwarding with delegate calls does not directly prevent the programmer from
explicitly
forwarding a call that can modify the
owner
or
impl
fields.
For example, one of the forwarded
f_{i}
methods could indeed be
update(x)
, which allows the implementation pointer to be updated.
The safety of the example would then depend on the implementation of this method.
Using integrity security settings
T
(Trusted) and
U
(Untrusted), with
T\sqsubseteq U
, it would be natural for the
Proxy
interface
I^{P}
to declare
impl
as
\text{var}(I^{X},T)
.
However, if
X.update
were declared simply as

⬇

func

update
(
x
)

{

this
.
impl

:=

x

}

thus permitting an unrestricted information flow into
impl
, the appropriate security setting in the implementation interface
I^{X}
would be to declare

⬇

impl

:

\text{var}(I^{X},U)

update

:

\text{proc}((I^{X},U))\texttt{:}U

In that case, forwarding
Proxy.update
to
X.update
with a delegate call would lead to an information flow from an Untrusted variable to a Trusted field, which is disallowed by the type settings and would lead to a stuck configuration in the type semantics (cf. rule
).
Hence, this cannot be safe, and it would be rejected by (the semantic type rule corresponding to) Lemma
3.24
.

4.
Up-to techniques for semantic typing

To manually prove type safety for a stack
Q
w.r.t. some type environment
\Delta
and security level
s
, we must exhibit a typing interpretation
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
containing the triplet
(Q,\Delta,s)
.
A major disadvantage of this approach is that it requires us to check the conditions of a typing interpretation for all the states reachable from
(Q,\Delta,s)
in the type-lifted transition system (Definition
3.7
).
This poses some practical problems in light of our proposal to store typing interpretations beside contract code, to witness type safety of fallback functions.
First, the contract creator, wishing to demonstrate type safety of his untypable code, must be able to
build
a typing interpretation containing the code.
Second, a user, wishing to invoke a piece of untypable code and ensure that the invocation is type safe, must be able to
verify
that a given set of triplets indeed is a typing interpretation (this should preferably be doable by an automatic decision procedure, which can be called by the type checker itself, rather than requiring manual intervention).
Not only may these tasks be laborious to do manually; they may even be impossible, since typing interpretations can contain infinitely many triplets in limit cases.
Furthermore, to be practically useful, it must be decidable whether a given set
\mathcal{R}
in fact
is
a typing interpretation, which may not always be the case.

The problem is similar to that of proving bisimilarity
[
31
]
between two processes, by exhibiting a bisimulation relating them.
In that case, we also need to check that the bisimulation conditions hold for every pair in the candidate relation.
This problem is particularly well-known in the field of process calculi, such as the
\pi
-calculus
[
32
;
30
;
35
]
, where program equivalences such as bisimilarity are studied extensively; see e.g.
[
42
;
43
]
.
To alleviate this burden of proof, Pous and Sangiorgi
[
37
]
present a number of
up-to techniques

[
31
;
40
]
for proving bisimilarity.
The idea is that it is enough to prove that a relation
\mathcal{R}
relating two processes is
contained in
a bisimulation.
Pous and Sangiorgi present a general theory of how to prove soundness of an up-to technique,
and this relies on greatest fixed-points on complete lattices; it can therefore also be adapted to work with other coinductively defined objects, like our typing interpretations.
To the best of our knowledge, this is the first use of up-to techniques in a concrete, non-relational setting.

4.1.
A motivating example

⬇

1

contract

Y

:

I^{Y}_{s}

{

2

\vdots

3

func

fallback
()

{

4

if

id

=

f_{1}

\lor

\ldots

\lor

id

=

f_{h}

then

call

X
.
id
()
$value

else

call

X
.
f_{0}
()
$value

5

}

6

}

Figure 8.
An example where
id
is used as the method name.

Consider the body of the fallback function in Figure
8
.
Calling methods by using the magic variable
id
makes the body untypable by the semantic type rules, as we have previously argued when discussing (
\dagger
).
However, the body consists of just a single
if-else
statement, with a method call in each branch.
Thus, this is a variant of the pattern presented in Figure
5
;
similarly to that example, also here, after just two transition steps, the body on the stack reduces to the body of one of the methods
f_{1},\ldots,f_{h}
or of the default handler
f_{0}
.
In terms of the type-lifted transition system, this body will yield a transition sequence of the form

\displaystyle\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{T}

\displaystyle\vDash(\texttt{if \ldots{} };\bot,\Delta,s_{1})\Mapsto(\texttt{call \ldots{} };s_{1};\bot,\Delta,s_{2})\Mapsto(S_{i};(\text{env}_{V},\Delta);s_{2};s_{1};\bot,\Delta_{i},s_{i})

where
S_{i}
is the body of the called handler method
f_{i}
,
\Delta_{i}
is the constructed type environment, and
s_{i}\sqsupseteq s_{2}\sqsupseteq s_{1}
.
If we further assume that all the handler methods
f_{i}
in fact are semantically typable, then there exists a typing interpretation
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{i}
containing
(S_{i};\bot,\Delta_{i},s_{i})
.
From this and the above, we can obtain that
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta_{i};\text{env}_{T}\vDash S_{i};(\text{env}_{V},\Delta);s_{2};s_{1};\bot:\text{cmd}(s_{i})
; thus, there exists a typing interpretation
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{i^{\prime}}
containing
(S_{i};(\text{env}_{V},\Delta);s_{2};s_{1};\bot,\Delta_{i},s_{i})
.

A typing interpretation for the body of the fallback function must contain the three triplets mentioned above for each of the possible other method calls
f_{i}
and the union of all the typing interpretations
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{i^{\prime}}
.
The union of a collection of typing interpretations is itself a typing interpretation, so we can let
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{\prime}\triangleq\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{0^{\prime}}\cup\ldots\cup\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{h^{\prime}}
and thus obtain a typing interpretation containing all the
continuations
after the method call is issued.
However, the problem is that the set

\mathcal{S}\triangleq\bigcup_{i\in\{0,\ldots,h\}}\left\{\,(\texttt{if \ldots{} };\bot,\Delta,s_{1}),(\texttt{call \ldots{} };s_{1};\bot,\Delta,s_{2}),(S_{i};(\text{env}_{V},\Delta);s_{2};s_{1};\bot,\Delta_{i},s_{i})\,\right\},

consisting of the union of the three triplets for each of the
f_{i}
handler methods, is
not
on its own a typing interpretation.
Hence, we cannot just discard
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{\prime}
and only take
\mathcal{S}
as our witness of type safety of the fallback function.
We need to take the whole union of
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{\prime}
, and likewise, any user wishing to ascertain type safety of a call invoking this fallback function would therefore also need to
check
this whole union.
This is impractical, as we can clearly see that, if
(Q^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime},s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}
and
\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{T}\vDash(Q^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime},s^{\prime})\Mapsto(Q^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime})
, then
(Q^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{\prime}
.
Thus, although
\mathcal{S}
is not itself a typing interpretation, it is a typing interpretation
up-to union
with
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{\prime}
.
Hence, we can use it in place of the full typing interpretation and let the remainder of the stack (viz.,
S_{i};(\text{env}_{V},\Delta);s_{2};s_{1};\bot
) be typed by the ordinary semantic type rules.
Using
\mathcal{S}
in place of
\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{\prime}
will drastically reduce the number of triplets that must be checked; this makes it easier for the contract creator to build the set
\mathcal{S}
and for the user to verify it.

4.2.
Typing interpretations up-to union

Following
37
, we shall formulate up-to techniques for typing interpretations in terms of progressions, which we define as follows:

Definition 4.1 (Progression).

Given environments
\Sigma
,
\Gamma
and
\text{env}_{T}
, and two sets of stack type triplets
\mathcal{S},\mathcal{R}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
, we say that
\mathcal{S}

progresses
to
\mathcal{R}
, written
\mathcal{S}\rightarrowtail\mathcal{R}
, if
(Q,\Delta,s)\in\mathcal{S}
implies

(1)

Q\in\mathcal{TQ}
, or

(2)

\exists P_{1}^{\prime}
such that
\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{T}\vDash(Q,\Delta,s)\Mapsto P_{1}^{\prime}
and

\forall P_{2}^{\prime}
such that
\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{T}\vDash(Q,\Delta,s)\Mapsto P_{2}^{\prime}
it holds that
P_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}
.

Note the similarity to the definition of typing interpretations (Definition
3.8
).
When
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{R}
coincide, we have the ordinary definition of a typing interpretation; thus
\mathcal{S}\rightarrowtail\mathcal{S}
if and only if
\mathcal{S}
is a typing interpretation.
The purpose of an up-to technique is to allow us to infer
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\nu\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
from a progression
\mathcal{S}\rightarrowtail\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{R}
, where
\mathcal{R}
is a set of redundant triplets.
If this is possible, then the up-to technique is
valid
(sound); this is the third cornestone result of our paper (for a proof, see Appendix
).

Definition 4.2 (Typing interpretation up-to union).

Let
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
be a typing interpretation w.r.t. environments
\Sigma
,
\Gamma
and
\text{env}_{T}
.
A set of stack type triplets
\mathcal{S}
is a
typing interpretation up-to union
with
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
if
\mathcal{S}\rightarrowtail\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
.

Theorem 4.3 (Validity of up-to union).

Let
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
be a typing interpretation.
If
\mathcal{S}
is a typing interpretation up-to union with
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
then
\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
is a typing interpretation.

Going back to the example in Section
4.1
, this means that we indeed can use the set
\mathcal{S}
in place of the full typing interpretation
\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{\prime}
to witness type safety of the body of the fallback function.
Instead of storing
\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{\prime}
on the blockchain, it will therefore suffice to store just
\mathcal{S}
, together with an indication that
\mathcal{S}
is a typing interpretation up-to union with the typing interpretation corresponding to the semantic type judgments

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta_{i};\text{env}_{T}\vDash S_{i};(\text{env}_{V},\Delta);s_{2};s_{1};\bot:\text{cmd}(s_{i})
which can be concluded by the ordinary semantic type rules.
This greatly reduces the number of triplets that must be stored in the blockchain and checked by (the type checker of) the user.

5.
Concluding remarks

The difference between safe and unsafe usages of the fallback function is not a purely syntactic property; this makes the conventional syntactic approach to type soundness unsuitable to handle this construct.
Hence, in this paper, we have instead followed the
semantic
approach to type soundness, by defining a semantics of types and type judgments in terms of a typed operational semantics, which gives us a definition of type safety that does not depend on syntactic type rules.
To coin a slogan, one might say that semantic typing is a
type-safety-first
approach.

The fallback function is a particularly problematic programming construct, both in terms of the security breaches to which it has given rise and the challenges it poses for a type-theoretic approach to ensuring program safety.
We have studied this construct in detail in the context of
TinySol
and security types, and proposed a solution to the problem of how to recover type safety of programs with fallback functions, without simply rendering the construct useless by preventing its execution altogether.
However, these developments should only be seen as an example of the approach we advocate; namely, semantic typing, coupled with up-to techniques and witnessing typing interpretations stored on the blockchain, are a means to ensure type safety even for syntactically untypable constructs.
This approach may be equally relevant for other notions of type safety (for another example, see Appendix
), other programming constructs, and even other smart-contract languages besides
TinySol
/Solidity.

We remark that our proposal is not the only possible way to formulate a semantics of types.
For example, in
[
13
]
, Caires gives a
logical
characterisation of various notions of type safety in the
\pi
-calculus by expressing types as formulae in the language of spatial logics
[
10
;
11
;
12
]
.
This allows him to directly define the sets of safe programs (processes) w.r.t. a given type formula, corresponding to our typing interpretations, as simply those processes that satisfy the formula.
This naturally poses the question of what kind of logic might be appropriate for describing secure information flows.
Proposals for such logics are presented in, e.g.,
[
5
;
7
;
20
;
22
;
45
]
.
Studying a logic suitable for describing the Volpano-Irvine-Smith type system
[
48
]
and developing a corresponding logical characterisation of the present notion of safety are interesting areas for future work.

The adaptation of up-to techniques from Section
4
is interesting, as it also opens new research avenues.
Indeed, their use is not limited to our present
TinySol
setting: such techniques are generally relevant for the semantic approach to type soundness based on typing interpretations.
Besides up-to union, Pous and Sangiorgi
[
37
]
also define a number of other up-to techniques for bisimulations which also should be transferable to typing interpretations, since they pertain to the coinduction proof method, and not only to bisimulations.
We can consider, for example,
up-to environment extension
, which could be relevant if runtime contract creation were added to
TinySol
.
Indeed, in our present setting,
\Sigma
,
\Gamma
and
\text{env}_{T}
are assumed to be static, but in the future we can consider a more realistic setting where new contracts can be dynamically added.
It would therefore be important to ensure that the addition of a new contract to the blockchain cannot invalidate a typing interpretation that witnesses the safety of an existing contract.

Last, but by no means least, it would be interesting to develop algorithms and tools for building and certifying typing interpretations, and to apply them to case studies from the domain of smart contracts that involve the use of the fallback function.

Data-Availability Statement

The goal of this paper is to develop the theoretical foundations needed to apply our ideas to the setting of smart-contract languages. As the technical contents of our article indicate, doing so required a substantial amount of technical work and we had no student support to build a prototype implementation. Therefore, at the moment, no artifact is available. We note, moreover, that the construction of tools for building and certifying typing interpretations requires the development of novel algorithms or the adaptions of existing ones for bisimilarity. As we state in the last paragraph of the conclusions (see ll. 1122-1124), this is an avenue for further work, which we plan to explore in the near future. Since we are still looking for students who might be interested in supporting us in this enterprise, it is quite unlikely that we shall be able to submit any artifact by the deadline for their submission.

The semantics for stacks provides a small-step semantics for commands; most of the rules are standard and reported in Figure
11
.
Note that, as we are using a typed syntax, with types appearing in declarations of local variables, we need a place to store these type assignments after the declaration has been performed, and the variable and its current value has been appended to the current variable environment
\text{env}_{V}
.
Thus, we record this type information directly in
\text{env}_{V}
along with the value, as is done in
[
1
]
.
This has no practical significance for the semantics, but is necessary for the syntactic type system (given in Appendix
C
), since variable environments can be pushed onto the stack in method calls, and recovered from the stack after a method returns (see rule
in Figure
).

The rules for the three forms of method calls in Figure
12
also deserve a few comments:
For rule
, we first evaluate the address and the parameters (viz.,
e_{1}
,
\widetilde{e}
and
e_{2}
), relatively to the current execution environment
\text{env}_{SV}
; we use the obtained address
Y
of the callee to retrieve the field environment
\text{env}_{F}^{Y}
for this contract and, through the method table, to extract the list of formal parameters
\widetilde{x}
and the body of the method
S
.
Also, we have to check that the number of actual parameters is the same as the number of formal parameters.
We then evaluate the currency amount passed through the call
6
6

6

Notice that here we should also check that the amount of currency transferred in the call (viz.,
z
in such rules) is at most the value currently owned by the caller in its
balance
field; if this is not the case, the method invocation fails and an exception is raised. To model this, we would need to complicate the semantics with an issue (namely, exception handling) that is orthogonal to the aims of this paper. Hence, we prefer to keep the presentation simple and let method invocations succeed, irrespectively of the amount of currency associated with them.

and
update the state environment by subtracting
z
from the balance of
X
and adding
z
to the balance of
Y
, in their respective field environments; this yields a new state
\text{env}_{S}^{\prime\prime}
.
We create the new execution environment
\text{env}_{V}^{\prime\prime}
by creating new bindings for the special variables
this
,
sender
and
value
, and by binding the formal parameters
\widetilde{x}
to the values of the actual parameters
\widetilde{v}
.
Finally, we execute the statement
S
in this new environment.
This yields the new state
\text{env}_{S}^{\prime}
, and also an updated variable environment
\text{env}_{V}^{\prime}
, since
S
may have modified the bindings in
\text{env}_{V}^{\prime\prime}
.
However, these bindings are local to the method and therefore we discard them once the call finishes.
So, the result of this transition is the updated state (
\text{env}_{S}^{\prime}
) and the original variable environment of the caller (
\text{env}_{V}
).

Rule
handles the fallback function. Essentially, this is a normal call, with the exception that it is activated when the invoked method is not provided by the callee.
Moreover, the magic variables
id
and
args
are bound to the invoked method name and to the actual arguments passed, respectively,.

Rule
accounts for delegate calls, which differ from normal calls in two aspects.
Since the method body
S
is executed in the context of the caller, the magic variables
this
,
sender
and
value
are
not
rebound and funds are not transferred to the callee with this type of call.
This is consistent with the idea that the code is executed in the context of the caller.

[
call
]

\dfrac{\left<e_{1},\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow Y\quad\left<e_{2},\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow z\quad\left<\widetilde{e},\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow\widetilde{v}}{\left<\texttt{call $e_{1}$.$m$($\widetilde{e}$)\$$e_{2}$};Q,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>\rightarrow\left<S;\text{env}_{V};Q,\text{env}_{T},\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime}\right>}

where:

~=(\texttt{this},Y),(\texttt{sender},X),(\texttt{value},z),(\texttt{id},f),(\texttt{args},\widetilde{v})

~=\text{env}_{S}\kern-2.0pt\left[X\mapsto\text{env}_{F}^{X}[\texttt{balance -= }z]\right]\kern-2.0pt\left[Y\mapsto\text{env}_{F}^{Y}[\texttt{balance += }z]\right]\par

[
dcall
]

Appendix B
Subtyping and type environment consistency rules

\Sigma
gives a
static
representation of the subtype-supertype relationship for interfaces.
We shall therefore need a way to decide whether a given
\Sigma
actually is
consistent
w.r.t. this relationship.
Thus, we shall give a set of rules for deciding whether a given interface
I_{1}
, declaring that it inherits from another interface
I_{2}
, indeed also is a
subtype
of
I_{2}
.
The rules are given in Figure
13
.

[
\Sigma
-top
]

\dfrac{\phantom{P}}{\Sigma;\Gamma\vdash(I^{\texttt{top}},I^{\texttt{top}})}\par

[
\Sigma
-rec
]

\dfrac{\Sigma;\Gamma\vdash\Sigma^{\prime}\quad\forall n\in\dom\left(\Gamma_{2}\right)\;.\;\Sigma\vdash\Gamma_{1}(n)\mathrel{<:}\Gamma_{2}(n)}{\Sigma;\Gamma\vdash(I_{1},I_{2}),\Sigma^{\prime}}

where:

I_{1}

~\neq I_{2}\qquad\not\exists I^{\prime}\;.\;\Sigma^{\prime}(I^{\prime})=I_{1}

\Gamma(I_{1})

~=\Gamma_{1}\qquad\Gamma(I_{2})=\Gamma_{2}\qquad\qquad\dom\left(\Gamma_{2}\right)\subseteq\dom\left(\Gamma_{1}\right)

Figure 13.
Consistency rules for
\Sigma
.

The judgment is of the form
\Sigma;\Gamma\vdash\Sigma
, which expresses that the inheritance tree recorded in
\Sigma
is
consistent
with the actual declarations recorded in
\Gamma
.
The rules are quite simple, since they just iterate through the inheritance environment, examining each entry in turn.

\Sigma
only records the inheritance hierarchy, whereas the actual structures of the interfaces are recorded in
\Gamma
.
Hence, consistency of any sub-part of
\Sigma
must be judged relative to
\Gamma
, as well as relative to the full
\Sigma
.

•

The base case is the rule
, since we assume the interface
I^{\texttt{top}}
always exists and inherits from nothing besides itself.
Furthermore, the form of the conclusion ensures that this rule can only be applied to an inheritance tree consisting of the root node itself.

•

The recursive case is the rule
, which examines the entry
(I_{1},I_{2})
.
In the side condition, we require that
I_{1}\neq I_{2}
, since no interface except
I^{\texttt{top}}
may inherit from itself.
We also require that there must not exist another interface
I^{\prime}
such that
I^{\prime}
inherits from
I_{1}
according to the remainder of the inheritance environment
\Sigma^{\prime}
.
This ensures that
is only applicable to a
leaf node
of the current inheritance tree, which is then trimmed away as we recur in the premise.
Hence it ensures that the environment indeed describes a
tree
.
Finally, the premise of
invokes a subtyping judgment

\Sigma\vdash\Gamma_{1}(n)\mathrel{<:}\Gamma_{2}(n)

for each member of the interface
I_{2}
, i.e. the supertype; the side condition

\dom\left(\Gamma_{2}\right)\subseteq\dom\left(\Gamma_{1}\right)

ensures that all members of the supertype also exist in the subtype.

The rules defining the subtyping judgments for interface members are
and
, given in Section
2.2
.
Both rules invoke a subtyping judgment for types of the form

\Sigma\vdash B_{1}\mathrel{<:}B_{2}

in their premise: this, finally, is the proper subtyping relation, which is defined by the rules in Figure
14
.
We shall also need this relation later in the presentation.
Note that we shall sometimes use the abbreviation

\Sigma\vdash B_{1}\mathrel{<:}B_{2}\mathrel{<:}B_{3}\triangleq\Sigma\vdash B_{1}\mathrel{<:}B_{2}\land\Sigma\vdash B_{2}\mathrel{<:}B_{3}

to simplify some premises.

[
sub-refl
]

\dfrac{\phantom{P}}{\Sigma\vdash B\mathrel{<:}B}\par

[
sub-trans
]

\dfrac{\Sigma\vdash B_{1}\mathrel{<:}B_{2}\quad\Sigma\vdash B_{2}\mathrel{<:}B_{3}}{\Sigma\vdash B_{1}\mathrel{<:}B_{3}}\par

[
sub-name
]

\dfrac{\phantom{P}}{\Sigma\vdash I_{1}\mathrel{<:}I_{2}}\;\left(\Sigma(I_{1})=I_{2}\right)

Figure 14.
The subtyping relation for expression types
B_{s}

Appendix C
Syntactic type rules

In our presentation, we intentionally left the syntactic category of operations
\op
undefined, and simply assumed that they can all be evaluated using some semantics
\rightarrow_{\op}
, such that
\op(\widetilde{v})\rightarrow_{\op}v
.
Now, we also assume that for each operation we can determine its signature, written
\vdash\op:\widetilde{B}\to B
.
To tie these two together, we need to make one further assumption;

Definition C.1 (Safety requirement for operations).

For all operations
\op
and argument list
\widetilde{v}
, if

•

\vdash\op:\widetilde{B}\to B
, and

•

|\widetilde{B}|=|\widetilde{v}|
, and

•

\forall v_{i}\in\widetilde{v}\;.\;\text{{TypeOf}}_{\Gamma}(v_{i})=(B_{i},s_{i})
,

then
\op(\widetilde{v})\rightarrow_{\op}v
and
\text{{TypeOf}}_{\Gamma}(v)=(B,s_{\bot})
, assuming any addresses
X
appearing in the argument list
\widetilde{v}
has an entry in
\Gamma
.

Note that, by this definition, we allow addresses to appear as arguments to operations.
However, as we assume that an operation can never
yield
an address, we also know that the resulting security level will be
s_{\bot}
and that the resulting base type
B
will be one of
int
or
bool
.

[
t-val
]

\dfrac{\Sigma\vdash B^{\prime}\mathrel{<:}B}{\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash v:B_{s}}\;\left(\begin{array}[]{r @{~} l}\text{{TypeOf}}_{\Gamma}(v)&=(B^{\prime},s^{\prime})\\
s^{\prime}&\sqsubseteq s\end{array}\right)\par

[
t-var
]

\dfrac{\Sigma\vdash B^{\prime}\mathrel{<:}B}{\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash x:B_{s}}\;\left(\begin{array}[]{r @{~} l}\Delta(x)&=\text{var}(B^{\prime},s^{\prime})\\
s^{\prime}&\sqsubseteq s\end{array}\right)\par

[
t-field
]

\dfrac{\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash e:I_{s}\quad\Sigma\vdash B^{\prime}\mathrel{<:}B}{\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash e.p:B_{s}}\;\left(\begin{array}[]{r @{~} l}\Gamma(I)(p)&=\text{var}(B^{\prime},s^{\prime})\\
s^{\prime}&\sqsubseteq s\end{array}\right)\par

[
t-op
]

\dfrac{\vdash\op:\widetilde{B}\to B\quad\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash\widetilde{e}:\widetilde{B}_{s,\ldots,s}}{\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash\op(\widetilde{e}):B_{s}}\par

Appendix D
Equivalence of states

The notion of an
s
-parametrised equivalence relation on states, written
\Gamma;\Delta\vdash\text{env}_{SV}^{1}=_{s}\text{env}_{SV}^{2}
, is defined by the rules in Figure
19
.
It denotes that the environments
\text{env}_{SV}^{1}
and
\text{env}_{SV}^{2}
have the same values on all entries of level
s
or lower.

[
eq-env
{}_{V}^{\emptyset}
]

\dfrac{\phantom{P}}{\Delta\vdash\text{env}_{V}^{\emptyset}=_{s}\text{env}_{V}^{\emptyset}}\par

[
eq-env
V
]

\dfrac{\Delta\vdash\text{env}_{V}^{1}=_{s}\text{env}_{V}^{2}}{\Delta\vdash\text{env}_{V}^{1},x:v_{1}=_{s}\text{env}_{V}^{2},x:v_{2}}\;\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\Delta(x)=\text{var}(B_{s^{\prime}})\\
s^{\prime}\sqsubseteq s\implies v_{1}=v_{2}\end{array}\right)\par

[
eq-env
{}_{F}^{\emptyset}
]

\dfrac{\phantom{P}}{\Gamma\vdash_{I}\text{env}_{F}^{\emptyset}=_{s}\text{env}_{F}^{\emptyset}}\par

[
eq-env
F
]

\dfrac{\Gamma\vdash_{I}\text{env}_{F}^{1}=_{s}\text{env}_{F}^{2}}{\Gamma\vdash_{I}\text{env}_{F}^{1},p:v_{1}=_{s}\text{env}_{F}^{2},p:v_{2}}\;\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\Gamma(I)(p)=\text{var}(B_{s^{\prime}})\\
s^{\prime}\sqsubseteq s\implies v_{1}=v_{2}\end{array}\right)\par

[
eq-env
{}_{S}^{\emptyset}
]

\dfrac{\phantom{P}}{\Gamma\vdash\text{env}_{S}^{\emptyset}=_{s}\text{env}_{S}^{\emptyset}}\par

[
eq-env
S

[
eq-env
SV
]

\dfrac{\Gamma\vdash\text{env}_{S}^{1}=_{s}\text{env}_{S}^{2}\quad\Delta\vdash\text{env}_{V}^{1}=_{S}\text{env}_{V}^{2}}{\Gamma;\Delta\vdash\text{env}_{SV}^{1}=_{s}\text{env}_{SV}^{2}}

Rules for the

Appendix E
Environment consistency

Having a well-typed stack
Q
and well-typed environments
\text{env}_{TSV}
is unfortunately not enough to ensure that the combination
\left<Q,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>
will also be safe to execute.
The names appearing in a stack
Q
must obviously also appear within the state
\text{env}_{SV}
, since otherwise the transition might become stuck.
However, well-typedness does not ensure the
existence
of a field or variable in
\text{env}_{SV}
, so it is entirely possible that two different states
\text{env}_{SV}^{1}
and
\text{env}_{SV}^{2}
can both be well-typed relative to the same
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta
, yet nevertheless have no fields or variables in common.
Then e.g.
\left<Q,\text{env}_{TSV}^{1}\right>
might be executable, but
\left<Q,\text{env}_{TSV}^{2}\right>
might not, if the two states do not contain the same names.

Likewise,
\text{env}_{T}
and
\text{env}_{S}
represent two different parts of the contract declarations, namely the method declarations and the field declarations, respectively.
Yet, well-typedness itself does not ensure that an arbitrary
\text{env}_{T}
and
\text{env}_{S}
are actually derived from the
same
set of contract declarations.

Furthermore, addresses can not only appear in the domain of
\text{env}_{S}
, but also as
values

v
in
\text{env}_{SV}
, e.g. as the value of a variable
x
.
Any such address could be ‘dereferenced’ if
x
is used in an expression or as the object path in a method call.
Well-typedness only ensures that the address has an appropriate interface type, but not that an appropriate
implementation
of the interface also exists on that address.
Moreover, even if a contract
C
declares that it implements an interface
I
, well-typedness does not ensure that
C
implements exactly
every member
of
I
.
It only ensures that the members that
C

does
implement are in accordance with the corresponding type definitions in
I
.

To handle these problems, we shall need to impose some further consistency checks on the environments
\text{env}_{TSV}
and their inner environments
\text{env}_{M}
and
\text{env}_{F}
, containing the methods and fields of each individual contract.
Firstly, we shall define two different notions of free names:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{V}(v)

\displaystyle=\emptyset

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{V}(x)

\displaystyle=\left\{\,x\,\right\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{V}(e.p)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{V}(e)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{V}(\op(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{V}(e_{1})\cup\ldots\cup\mathcal{F}_{V}(e_{n})

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{V}(\texttt{skip})

\displaystyle=\emptyset

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{V}(\texttt{throw})

\displaystyle=\emptyset

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{V}(\texttt{var $x$ := $e$ in $S$})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{V}(e)\cup\left(\mathcal{F}_{V}(S)\mathbin{\backslash}\left\{\,x\,\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{V}(\texttt{$x$ := $e$})

\displaystyle=\left\{\,x\,\right\}\cup\mathcal{F}_{V}(e)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{V}(\texttt{this.$p$ := $e$})

\displaystyle=\left\{\,\texttt{this}\,\right\}\cup\mathcal{F}_{V}(e)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{V}(\texttt{$S_{1}$; $S_{2}$})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{V}(S_{1})\cup\mathcal{F}_{V}(S_{2})

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{V}(\texttt{if $e$ then $S_{1}$ else $S_{2}$})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{V}(e)\cup\mathcal{F}_{V}(S_{1})\cup\mathcal{F}_{V}(S_{2})

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{V}(\texttt{while $e$ do $S$})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{V}(e)\cup\mathcal{F}_{V}(S)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{V}(\texttt{call $e_{1}$.$m$($e_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,e_{n}^{\prime}$)\$$e_{2}$})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{V}(e_{1})\cup\mathcal{F}_{V}(e_{1}^{\prime})\cup\ldots\cup\mathcal{F}_{V}(e_{n}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\quad\cup\mathcal{F}_{V}(e_{2})\cup\mathcal{F}_{V}(m)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{V}(\texttt{dcall $e$.$m$($e_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,e_{n}^{\prime}$)})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{V}(e)\cup\mathcal{F}_{V}(e_{1}^{\prime})\cup\ldots\cup\mathcal{F}_{V}(e_{n}^{\prime})\cup\left\{\,\texttt{id}\,\right\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{V}(m)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\left\{\,\texttt{id}\,\right\}&\text{if $m$ = {id}}\\
\emptyset&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Figure 20.
The free variable names for expressions and statements.

Definition E.1 (Free names).

We use the following notation:

•

We write
\mathcal{F}_{A}(S)
and
\mathcal{F}_{A}(e)
for the set of
addresses

X
occurring in a statement
S
and an expression
e
, respectively.
We omit a formal definition, since addresses cannot be bound, so they can be read directly from the syntax.

•

We write
\mathcal{F}_{V}(S)
and
\mathcal{F}_{V}(e)
for the set of
free variable names

x
occurring in a statement
S
and an expression
e
.
The formal definition is given in Figure
20
.
We assume here that
x
also ranges over the magic variable names
this
,
sender
,
value
,
id
, and
args
.

[

\dfrac{\phantom{P}}{\text{env}_{S}\vdash\text{env}_{F}^{\emptyset}\rightleftharpoons\emptyset}\par

where:

v\in\mathcal{N}_{A}

~\implies v\in\dom\left(\text{env}_{S}\right)\par

where:

\mathcal{F}_{A}(S)

~\subseteq\dom\left(\text{env}_{S}\right)

Figure 21.
Consistency rules for
\text{env}_{F}
and
\text{env}_{M}
.

Now, in Figure
21
, we give the rules for a consistency check for
\text{env}_{F}
and
\text{env}_{M}
.
Both are defined in terms of a relation
\rightleftharpoons
between a code environment (
\text{env}_{F}
, resp.
\text{env}_{M}
) and type environment (
\Gamma_{F}
, resp.
\Gamma_{M}
), where the latter represents the interface definitions of fields and methods for a particular contract.
For
J\in\left\{\,F,M\,\right\}
, the consistency judgments are of the form

\text{env}_{S}\vdash\text{env}_{J}\rightleftharpoons\Gamma_{J}.

In rule
we check that each field definition in
\Gamma_{F}
has a corresponding field declaration in
\text{env}_{F}
; furthermore, if the value
v
stored in the field
p
is an address, then this address must also be in the domain of
\text{env}_{S}
.
The first check ensures that all declared fields also have an implementation, whilst the second check ensures that all addresses appearing as values also contain a contract.
In rule
we check that each method signature has a corresponding implementation.
Furthermore, in the side condition, we require that any free addresses occurring in the method body
S
must also exist in
\text{env}_{S}
.
This ensures that, even if a ‘hard-coded’ address appears in
S
, there must still be a contract on that address.
This is necessary, since well-typedness alone only ensures that the address has the correct type for its usage, but not that it contains an implementation.

[
c-envv
1
]

\dfrac{\phantom{P}}{\text{env}_{S}\vdash\text{env}_{V}^{\emptyset}}\par

[
c-envv
2
]

\dfrac{\text{env}_{S}\vdash\text{env}_{V}}{\text{env}_{S}\vdash\text{env}_{V},x:v}

where:

v\in\mathcal{N}_{A}

~\implies v\in\dom\left(\text{env}_{S}\right)\par

[
c-envs
1
]

\dfrac{\phantom{P}}{\Gamma;\text{env}_{S}\vdash\text{env}_{S}^{\emptyset}}\par

[
c-envs
2
]

\dfrac{\Gamma;\text{env}_{S}\vdash\text{env}_{S}^{\prime}\quad\text{env}_{S}\vdash\text{env}_{F}\rightleftharpoons\Gamma_{I}|_{\mathcal{N}_{F}}}{\Gamma;\text{env}_{S}\vdash\text{env}_{S}^{\prime},X:\text{env}_{F}}\;\left(\begin{array}[]{r @{~} l}\Gamma(X)&=I_{s}\\
\Gamma(I)&=\Gamma_{I}\end{array}\right)\par

[
c-envt
1
]

\dfrac{\phantom{P}}{\Gamma;\text{env}_{S}\vdash\text{env}_{T}^{\emptyset}}\par

[
c-envt
2
]

\dfrac{\Gamma;\text{env}_{S}\vdash\text{env}_{T}^{\prime}\quad\text{env}_{S}\vdash\text{env}_{M}\rightleftharpoons\Gamma_{I}|_{\mathcal{N}_{M}}}{\Gamma;\text{env}_{S}\vdash\text{env}_{T}^{\prime},X:\text{env}_{M}}\;\left(\begin{array}[]{r @{~} l}\Gamma(X)&=I_{s}\\
\Gamma(I)&=\Gamma_{I}\end{array}\right)\par

[
c-envsv
]

\dfrac{\Gamma;\text{env}_{S}\vdash\text{env}_{S}\quad\text{env}_{S}\vdash\text{env}_{V}}{\Gamma\vdash\text{env}_{SV}}\par
