]

\dfrac{\Gamma;\text{env}_{S}\vdash\text{env}_{T}\quad\Gamma;\text{env}_{S}\vdash\text{env}_{S}\quad\text{env}_{S}\vdash\text{env}_{V}}{\Gamma\vdash\text{env}_{TSV}}

where:

\dom\left(\text{env}_{T}\right)

~=\dom\left(\text{env}_{S}\right)

Figure 22.
Consistency rules for the environments
\text{env}_{TSV}
.

Next, we can define the consistency rules for
\text{env}_{TSV}
, which are given in Figure
22
.
Note that we use the notation
f|_{\mathcal{D}}
to denote the
restriction
of the domain of a function
f
to the set of values in
\mathcal{D}
.
We use this to extract the field, resp. method, signatures from an inner type environment
\Gamma_{I}
, corresponding to the declaration of an interface
I
, in rules
and
, which then invoke the consistency rules from Figure
21
in their premises.
The rules are otherwise straightforward:

•

Rules
and
are used to check the consistency of the variable environment
\text{env}_{V}
, which simply consists of ensuring that any address appearing as a value also exists in the domain of
\text{env}_{S}
, similar to the rule
above.
Thus, the judgment is relative to
\text{env}_{S}
.

•

Rules
and
are used to check the consistency of
\text{env}_{S}
, which contains all field declarations of all contracts.
It must be judged consistent relative to
itself
, as well as to
\Gamma
, because we need to ensure both that all declared fields have an implementation,
and
that all addresses appearing as values refer to actual contracts.

•

Rules
and
are used to check the consistency of
\text{env}_{T}
, which contains all method declarations of all contracts.
Like
, we extract the relevant method declarations from
\Gamma
for each collection of declarations
\text{env}_{M}
, which then must correspond.

•

Finally, we have the two short-hand rules
and
, which are used to judge consistency of a collection of environments
\text{env}_{SV}
, resp.
\text{env}_{TSV}
, together.
Notably, in both rules,
\text{env}_{S}
must be judged consistent relative to
itself
; moreover, in the latter rule we also require that the domains of
\text{env}_{T}
and
\text{env}_{S}
must coincide.
This must be the case, if
\text{env}_{T}
and
\text{env}_{S}
are derived from the same collection of contracts.

Well-typedness and consistency together ensure that all addresses actually contain contracts with appropriate interface types, and that all contracts also implement all members of their interfaces.
Unless noted otherwise, in what follows, we shall only consider consistent environments
\text{env}_{TSV}
.

Appendix F
Semantic Typing for Expressions

The typed operational semantic for expressions relies on judgements of the form

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{B_{s}}\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v
.
This judgement should be read as:
e
evaluates to the value
v
of type
B_{s}
, or a subtype thereof, given
\text{env}_{SV}
and the type assumptions in
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta
.
The rules are in Figure
23
and have some quite complicated side conditions.
In particular, to ensure that the coercion property holds, all rules have a side condition of the form
s^{\prime}\sqsubseteq s
, which ensures that the transition can be concluded for any level
s
or
higher
.

[
r-val
]

\dfrac{\Sigma\vdash B^{\prime}\mathrel{<:}B}{\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{B_{s}}\left<v,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v}\;\left(\begin{array}[]{r @{~} l}\text{{TypeOf}}_{\Gamma}(v)&=(B^{\prime},s^{\prime})\\
s^{\prime}&\sqsubseteq s\end{array}\right)\par

[
r-var
]

\dfrac{\Sigma\vdash B_{1}\mathrel{<:}B_{2}\mathrel{<:}B}{\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{B_{s}}\left<x,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v}\;\left(\begin{array}[]{r @{~} l}\text{env}_{V}(x)&=v\\
\text{{TypeOf}}_{\Gamma}(v)&=(B_{1},s_{1})\\
\Delta(x)&=\text{var}(B_{2},s_{2})\\
s_{1}\sqsubseteq&s_{2}\sqsubseteq s\end{array}\right)\par

[
r-field
]

\dfrac{\Sigma\vdash B_{1}\mathrel{<:}B_{2}\mathrel{<:}B\qquad\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{I_{s}}\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow X}{\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{B_{s}}\left<e.p,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v}\;\left(\begin{array}[]{r @{~} l}\text{env}_{S}(X)(p)&=v\\
\text{{TypeOf}}_{\Gamma}(v)&=(B_{1},s_{1})\\
\Gamma(I)(p)&=\text{var}(B_{2},s_{2})\\
s_{1}\sqsubseteq&s_{2}\sqsubseteq s\end{array}\right)\par

[
r-op
]

\dfrac{\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{\widetilde{B}_{s,\ldots,s}}\left<\widetilde{e},\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow\widetilde{v}\quad\op(\widetilde{v})\rightarrow_{\op}v}{\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{B_{s}}\left<\op(\widetilde{e}),\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v}\;\left(\vdash\op:\widetilde{B}\to B\right)

Figure 23.
Typed operational semantics for expressions.

In
, we are evaluating a hard-coded value, so we use
\text{{TypeOf}}_{\Gamma}(\cdot)
to obtain its type.
However, as this value could be an
address

X
, and its type therefore would be an interface
I
, we need to invoke subtyping on the actual type, in case the runtime base type
B
in fact were a supertype of
I
, rather than
I
itself.

In
, we have a similar situation to the one in
, but now we need to invoke subtyping twice:
first, to ensure that the actual value
v
in fact was able to be stored in the variable
x
; second, to obtain the runtime base type
B
, in case
v
were an address and the runtime type were a supertype of its interface.
The check on the value is necessary, since we cannot know whether
\text{env}_{V}
in fact is well-typed, so we must check this at runtime as well whenever an entry is read.
Likewise, we must require that the three security levels form a chain
s_{1}\sqsubseteq s_{2}\sqsubseteq s
; first, we must have that
s_{1}\sqsubseteq s_{2}
to ensure that the value in fact was of a lower level than the container
x
; second, we must have that
s_{2}\sqsubseteq s
to ensure that the coercion property holds.

Rule
is similar to the situation in
, except that we must now, in addition, have that
e
evaluates to an address
X
of type
I_{s}
.
Thus, we require a runtime type
I_{s}
in the evaluation.
Now, because of subtyping and the coercion property, it might be the case that the
actual
type of
X
were some interface
I^{\prime}
and of level
s^{\prime}
such that
\Sigma\vdash I^{\prime}\mathrel{<:}I
and
s^{\prime}\sqsubseteq s
, but in that case the transition can be concluded for
I_{s}
as well.
Note that, by requiring the runtime level
s
in the evaluation of
e
, we are in effect ensuring that
e
can be evaluated to a level that is
lower
than, or equal to,
s
, which ascertains that higher-level parts of the program cannot alter the path to the field being read.

In
, we require the runtime types
\widetilde{B}_{s,\ldots s}
for the operands
\widetilde{v}
, and in the side condition we require that
\vdash\op:\widetilde{B}\to B
.
Thus, we make use of two assumptions: first, the safety requirement for operations (Definition
C.1
), which assures us that the resulting value
v
in fact will be of type
B
; second,
v
cannot be an address, since no operation is allowed to return an address value.
Hence, we can let the type of
v
, obtained from the signature of
\op
, equal the runtime base type, because
B
must be one of
int
or
bool
, on which subtyping can only be reflexive.
Furthermore, by Definition
2.4
, the level of
v
can only be
s_{\bot}
, and therefore it always holds that the level of
v
is below, or equal to, the runtime level
s
, since by definition
s_{\bot}\sqsubseteq s
.
Lastly, the runtime levels
s,\ldots,s
required for the operands are the same as the level
s
in the conclusion.
In effect, we thus require that all operands can be evaluated at
some
levels
s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}
, which must all be equal to, or lower than,
s
.

We now prove a few results about the types semantics of expressions.
The first two results state that every transition that can be concluded by the typed semantic rules can also be concluded by the ‘untyped’ semantics and that the coercion property for expressions (relying on the ordering of security levels and on the subtyping relation) holds.

Theorem F.1 (Semantic compatibility for expressions).

If
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{B_{s}}\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v
, then
\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v
.

Proof.

By induction on the inference for
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{B_{s}}\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v
.
There are two base cases:

•

Suppose
was used; then,
e=v
. As there are no premises or side conditions,
\left<v,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v
can then be concluded by
.

•

Suppose
was used; then,
e=x
and, from the side condition of this rule, we know that
\text{env}_{V}(x)=v
.
This satisfies the side condition of
, which then allows us to conclude
\left<x,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v
.

There are two inductive cases:

•

Suppose
was used; then
e=e^{\prime}.p
and, from the premise and side conditions, we know that

\displaystyle\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta

\displaystyle\vDash_{I_{s}}\left<e^{\prime},\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow X

\displaystyle\text{env}_{S}(X)(p)

\displaystyle=v

By the induction hypothesis, the first fact implies that

\left<e^{\prime},\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow X

This, together with
\text{env}_{S}(X)(p)=v
and rule
, allows us to conclude that
\left<e.p,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v
.

•

Suppose
was used; then,
e=\op(\widetilde{e})
and, from the premises of the rule, we have that

\displaystyle\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta

\displaystyle\vDash_{(B_{1},s)}\left<e_{1},\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v_{1}

\displaystyle\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta

\displaystyle\vDash_{(B_{n},s)}\left<e_{n},\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v_{n}

\displaystyle\op(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})\rightarrow_{\op}v

By the induction hypothesis on each of the
n
transitions, we obtain

\left<e_{1},\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v_{1}\qquad\ldots\qquad\left<e_{n},\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v_{n}

These, together with
\op(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})\rightarrow_{\op}v
and rule
, allow us to conclude that
\left<\op(\widetilde{e}),\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v
. ∎

Theorem F.2 (Coercion property for expressions).

If
\Sigma\vdash B_{1}\mathrel{<:}B_{2}
and
s_{1}\sqsubseteq s_{2}
, then

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{(B_{1},s_{1})}\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v
implies that

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{(B_{2},s_{2})}\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v
.

Proof.

By induction on the inference for
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{(B_{1},s_{1})}\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v
.
There are two base cases:

•

If
was used, then
e=v
; from the premise and side conditions, we know that

\displaystyle\text{{TypeOf}}_{\Gamma}(v)

\displaystyle=(B^{\prime},s^{\prime})

\displaystyle\Sigma

\displaystyle\vdash B^{\prime}\mathrel{<:}B_{1}

\displaystyle s^{\prime}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq s_{1}

By assumption,
\Sigma\vdash B_{1}\mathrel{<:}B_{2}
and
s_{1}\sqsubseteq s_{2}
, so, by transitivity, we have that

\displaystyle\Sigma

\displaystyle\vdash B^{\prime}\mathrel{<:}B_{2}

\displaystyle s^{\prime}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq s_{2}

This satisfies the premises of
, so we can conclude that

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{(B_{2},s_{2})}\left<v,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v

•

If
was used, then
e=x
; from the premise and side conditions, we know that

\displaystyle\text{env}_{V}(x)

\displaystyle=v

\displaystyle\text{{TypeOf}}_{\Gamma}(v)

\displaystyle=(B_{1}^{\prime},s_{1}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\Delta(x)

\displaystyle=\text{var}(B_{2}^{\prime},s_{2}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\Sigma

\displaystyle\vdash B_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{<:}B_{2}^{\prime}\mathrel{<:}B_{1}

\displaystyle s_{1}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq

\displaystyle\ s_{2}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq s_{1}

By assumption,
\Sigma\vdash B_{1}\mathrel{<:}B_{2}
and
s_{1}\sqsubseteq s_{2}
, so by transitivity we have that

\displaystyle\Sigma

\displaystyle\vdash B_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{<:}B_{2}^{\prime}\mathrel{<:}B_{2}

\displaystyle s_{1}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq

\displaystyle\ s_{2}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq s_{2}

This satisfies the premises of
, so we can conclude that

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{(B_{2},s_{2})}\left<x,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v

There are two inductive cases:

•

If
was used, then
e=e^{\prime}.p
; from the premise and side conditions, we know that

\displaystyle\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta

\displaystyle\vDash_{(I,s_{1})}\left<e^{\prime},\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow X

\displaystyle\text{env}_{S}(X)(p)

\displaystyle=v

\displaystyle\text{{TypeOf}}_{\Gamma}(v)

\displaystyle=(B_{1}^{\prime},s_{1}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\Gamma(I)(p)

\displaystyle=\text{var}(B_{2}^{\prime},s_{2}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\Sigma

\displaystyle\vdash B_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{<:}B_{2}^{\prime}\mathrel{<:}B_{1}

\displaystyle s_{1}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq

\displaystyle\ s_{2}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq s_{1}

By assumption,
\Sigma\vdash B_{1}\mathrel{<:}B_{2}
and
s_{1}\sqsubseteq s_{2}
, so by transitivity we have that

\displaystyle\Sigma

\displaystyle\vdash B_{2}^{\prime}\mathrel{<:}B_{2}

\displaystyle s_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq s_{2}

By the induction hypothesis,

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{(I,s_{2})}\left<e^{\prime},\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow X

can be concluded as well.
This satisfies the premises of
, so

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{(B_{2},s_{2})}\left<e^{\prime}.p,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v

•

If
was used, then
e=\op(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n})
; from the premise and side conditions, we know that

\displaystyle\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta

\displaystyle\vDash_{(B_{1}^{\prime},s_{1})}\left<e_{1},\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v_{1}

\displaystyle\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta

\displaystyle\vDash_{(B_{n}^{\prime},s_{1})}\left<e_{n},\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v_{n}

\displaystyle\op(\widetilde{v})\rightarrow_{\op}v

\displaystyle\vdash\op:B_{1}^{\prime},\ldots B_{n}^{\prime}\to B_{1}

By assumption,
s_{1}\sqsubseteq s_{2}
; thus, by the induction hypothesis,

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{(B_{1}^{\prime},s_{2})}\left<e_{1},\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v_{1}\qquad\ldots\qquad\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{(B_{n}^{\prime},s_{2})}\left<e_{n},\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v_{n}

Furthermore, no operation
\op
can yield any other type of value than
bool
or
int
, for which the only form of subtyping, that can be concluded, is by reflexivity (rule
).
Thus, since
\Sigma\vdash B_{1}\mathrel{<:}B_{2}
by assumption, we know that
B_{1}=B_{2}
.
Therefore,

\vdash\op:B_{1}^{\prime},\ldots B_{n}^{\prime}\to B_{2}

also holds.
This satisfies the premises of
, so

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{(B_{2},s_{2})}\left<\op(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}),\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v\qed

An expression configuration
\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}\right>
is
safe
precisely when it yields a value
v
by the typed semantics; i.e. the rules ensure both that
v
indeed is of type
B
, or a subtype thereof, and that all containers
read from
in the evaluation of
e
are of security level
s
or lower.
This precisely captures the intended meaning of safety implied by the expression type
B_{s}
.
Again, we can quite easily show that this property holds for transitions in the typed semantics:

Theorem F.3 (Runtime safety for expressions).

If
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{B_{s}}\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v
, then
\text{{TypeOf}}_{\Gamma}(v)=(B_{1},s_{1})
, for some
B_{1}
and
s_{1}
such that: (1)
\Sigma\vdash B_{1}\mathrel{<:}B
; (2)
s_{1}\sqsubseteq s
; and (3) every container read from in the evaluation of
e
is of type
\text{var}(B_{2},s_{2})
, with
s_{2}\sqsubseteq s
.

Proof.

By induction on the inference of
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{B_{s}}\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v
.
There are two base cases:

•

Suppose the transition was concluded by
; then,
e=v
. As no variables or fields are evaluated, the requirement on their security level is vacuously satisfied.
From the premise and side conditions, we directly obtain the desired

\displaystyle\text{{TypeOf}}_{\Gamma}(v)

\displaystyle=(B_{1},s_{1})

\displaystyle\Sigma

\displaystyle\vdash B_{1}\mathrel{<:}B

\displaystyle s_{1}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq s

•

Suppose the transition was concluded by
; then,
e=x
. From the premise and side conditions, we have that

\displaystyle\text{env}_{V}(x)

\displaystyle=v

\displaystyle\text{{TypeOf}}_{\Gamma}(v)

\displaystyle=(B_{1},s_{1})

\displaystyle\Delta(x)

\displaystyle=\text{var}(B_{2},s_{2})

\displaystyle\Sigma

\displaystyle\vdash B_{1}\mathrel{<:}B_{2}\mathrel{<:}B

\displaystyle s_{1}\sqsubseteq

\displaystyle\ s_{2}\sqsubseteq s

Hence, we read from a container, which has type
\text{var}(B_{2},s_{2})
for
s_{2}\sqsubseteq s
, as required.
Furthermore, we can conclude
s_{1}\sqsubseteq s
and
\Sigma\vdash B_{1}\mathrel{<:}B
as well by transitivity of the two relations.

There are two inductive cases:

•

Suppose the transition was concluded by
; then,
e=e^{\prime}.p
. From the premise and side conditions, we have that

\displaystyle\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta

\displaystyle\vDash_{I_{s}}\left<e^{\prime},\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow X

\displaystyle\text{env}_{S}(X)(p)

\displaystyle=v

\displaystyle\text{{TypeOf}}_{\Gamma}(v)

\displaystyle=(B_{1},s_{1})

\displaystyle\Gamma(I)(p)

\displaystyle=\text{var}(B_{2},s_{2})

\displaystyle\Sigma

\displaystyle\vdash B_{1}\mathrel{<:}B_{2}\mathrel{<:}B

\displaystyle s_{1}\sqsubseteq

\displaystyle s_{2}\sqsubseteq s

By the induction hypothesis, the statement of this Lemma holds for the transition

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{I_{s}}\left<e^{\prime},\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow X

In particular, all containers read from in the evaluation of
e^{\prime}
are of level
s
or lower.
Moreover, the field
p
has type
\text{var}(B_{2},s_{2})
, and so the requirement
s_{2}\sqsubseteq s
holds.
Finally, we can conclude
s_{1}\sqsubseteq s
and
\Sigma\vdash B_{1}\mathrel{<:}B
as well, by transitivity of the two relations.

•

Suppose the transition was concluded by
; then
e=\op(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n})
. From the premise and side conditions, we have that

\displaystyle\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta

\displaystyle\vDash_{(B_{1},s)}\left<e_{1},\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v_{1}

\displaystyle\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta

\displaystyle\vDash_{(B_{n},s)}\left<e_{n},\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v_{n}

\displaystyle\op(\widetilde{v})\rightarrow_{\op}v

\displaystyle\vdash\op:\widetilde{B}\to B

By
n
applications of the induction hypothesis, we know that the statement of this Lemma holds for the
n
transitions above.
Hence, no container of a level higher than
s
is read from in the evaluations of
e_{1}
, …,
e_{n}
.
Finally, we know that
\text{{TypeOf}}_{\Gamma}(v)=(B,s_{\bot})
by the safety requirement for operations (Definition
C.1
), and
s_{\bot}\sqsubseteq s
by definition. ∎

Corollary F.4 (Runtime non-interference for expressions).

If
s_{1}\sqsubseteq s_{2}
,
\Gamma;\Delta\vdash\text{env}_{SV}^{1}=_{s_{2}}\text{env}_{SV}^{2}
,

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{B_{s_{1}}}\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}^{1}\right>\rightarrow v_{1}
, and

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{B_{s_{1}}}\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}^{2}\right>\rightarrow v_{2}
,
then
v_{1}=v_{2}
.

Proof.

By assumption, all values of entries of level
s_{2}
or lower in
\text{env}_{SV}^{1}
and
\text{env}_{SV}^{2}
agree; thus, by Theorem
F.3
, only entries of level
s_{1}
or lower are read in the transitions, since
s_{1}\sqsubseteq s_{2}
. Thus, the two expressions use the same values and therefore also produce the same result.
∎

Theorem
F.3
ensures that, if an expression
e
can be evaluated at a given type, then the resulting value
v
will indeed be of that type.
However, suppose
v
is an address
X
.
It is then technically possible that
X
could have a type in
\Gamma
, but not actually contain an
implementation
in
\text{env}_{S}
.
This trivial error is easily prevented by also requiring well-typedness and consistency of
\text{env}_{SV}
, and that
e
does not contain any free names.

Corollary F.5 (Consistency of expression values).

If
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash\text{env}_{SV}
(well-typedness),
\Gamma\vdash\text{env}_{SV}
(consistency), and
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{B_{s}}\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v
,
then
v\in\mathcal{N}_{A}
implies that
v\in\dom\left(\text{env}_{S}\right)
.

Proof.

By Theorem
F.3
, we know that
\text{{TypeOf}}_{\Gamma}(v)=(B_{1},s_{1})
such that
\Sigma\vdash B_{1}\mathrel{<:}B
and
s_{1}\sqsubseteq s
.
Thus
v
is ensured to have a type.
If
v
is an address
X
, then by the definition of
\text{{TypeOf}}_{\Gamma}(\cdot)
(Definition
2.4
),
X
must be an interface type
I
from
\Gamma
.
Well-typedness and consistency of
\text{env}_{SV}
then together ensure that every interface
I
in
\Gamma
also must have an implementation in
\text{env}_{S}
.
Thus we conclude that
v\in\dom\left(\text{env}_{S}\right)
.
∎

We now show a result that allows us to abstract away from the specific values stored in the state environment
\text{env}_{SV}
.
This ensures that, if an expression
e
has a typed transition for some appropriately shaped
\text{env}_{SV}^{1}
, then any other similarly shaped
\text{env}_{SV}^{2}
will also yield a transition.
Indeed, given some
\Sigma
,
\Gamma
and
\Delta
, in Definition
3.2
we show how to construct environments
\text{env}_{SV}
such that well-typedness and consistency are ensured to hold, and thereby abstract away from a particular choice of these environments. Now, using the construction of Definition
3.2
, we can now abstract away from the specific values used in the evaluation of an expression configuration
\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}\right>
and thereby obtain a result about the safety of the expression
e
itself:

Theorem F.6 (Safe state abstraction).

For all
\Sigma
,
\Gamma
and
\Delta
, if
\text{env}_{SV}^{1}
and
\text{env}_{SV}^{2}
are built according to Definition
3.2
and

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{B_{s}}\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}^{1}\right>\rightarrow v_{1}
, then there exists a
v_{2}
such that

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{B_{s}}\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}^{2}\right>\rightarrow v_{2}
.

Proof.

By induction on the rules of the typed semantics (Figure
23
).
By construction,
\text{env}_{SV}^{1}
and
\text{env}_{SV}^{2}
contain the same named entries (fields and variables), so since the configuration
\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}^{1}\right>
was executable, the configuration
\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}^{2}\right>
cannot be stuck due to a missing variable name.
A field or variable in
\text{env}_{SV}^{2}
could contain an address
X
not present in
\text{env}_{S}^{1}
, but by Lemma
3.3
we know that
\text{env}_{SV}^{2}
is well-typed and consistent w.r.t.
\Sigma
,
\Gamma
and
\Delta
, which by Corollary
F.5
ensures that
X
must have an implementation in
\text{env}_{S}^{2}
, and by Theorem
F.3
that
X
has the appropriate type.
Thus, this cannot lead to the configuration being stuck either.
∎

The actual values
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
produced in the two transitions in Theorem
F.6
may of course differ, but by Theorem
F.3
above, their
types
will be the same.
This intuitively follows because the typed semantics only restricts the
types
of values read from
\text{env}_{SV}
, and not the actual values.
The existence of a typed transition with
\text{env}_{SV}^{1}
implies that all the values read from this environment in the evaluation of
e
are in accordance with the type restrictions in
\Sigma
,
\Gamma
,
\Delta
; and choosing any other state
\text{env}_{SV}^{2}
, which satisfies the same restrictions, will therefore still yield a transition.

Finally, Theorem
F.6
allow us to abstract away from any
specific
choice of environments
\text{env}_{SV}
and instead focus on expressions that have a transition for
any
sensible choice of these environments.
By this theorem, if an expression
e
has a transition for some
\text{env}_{SV}^{1}
constructed according to Definition
3.2
, then it will have a transition for
all
similarly constructed environments
\text{env}_{SV}^{2}
.
In a sense, these environments can be thought of as representing all meaningful ‘inputs’ to the expression and, for any such appropriately shaped input, a safe expression will yield a value of the correct type.
We can now define the set of such safe expressions by using the notion of
typing interpretations
in the style of Caires
[
13
]
:

Definition F.7 (Typing interpretation for expressions).

A
typing interpretation
for expressions under a given
\Sigma
,
\Gamma
,
\Delta
and
B_{s}
, written
\mathcal{R}^{B_{s}}_{\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta}
, is a set of expressions such that, if
e\in\mathcal{R}^{B_{s}}_{\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta}
, then there exist an
\text{env}_{SV}
built according to Definition
3.2
and a value
v
such that

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{B_{s}}\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v
.
The
typing
of expressions w.r.t. a given
\Sigma
,
\Gamma
,
\Delta
and
B_{s}
, written
\nu\mathcal{R}^{B_{s}}_{\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta}
, is the union of all typing interpretations
\mathcal{R}^{B_{s}}_{\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta}
.
We write
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash e:B_{s}
if there exists a typing interpretation
\mathcal{R}^{B_{s}}_{\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta}
containing
e
.

Intuitively, any expression from
\nu\mathcal{R}^{B_{s}}_{\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta}
will safely
behave as
a value of the type
B_{s}
according to the typed semantics, regardless of the actual values bound to its names (as long as they are of the appropriate types).
This is exactly what we would expect it to
mean
, when we say an expression inhabits the type
B_{s}
.
However, Definition
F.7
does not tell us how to prove that an expression
e
inhabits a given type
B_{s}
, apart from exhibiting a typing interpretation
\mathcal{R}^{B_{s}}_{\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta}
containing
e
.
To remedy this situation, we shall therefore now state and show a theorem, which expresses that the syntactic type system is compatible with Definition
F.7
, meaning
that typability implies safety.

Theorem F.8 (Compatibility for expressions).

If
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash e:B_{s}
, then
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash e:B_{s}
.

Proof.

We must show that
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash e:B_{s}
implies that there exists a typing interpretation
\mathcal{R}^{B_{s}}_{\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta}
containing
e
.
The simplest such typing interpretation is just the singleton set
\left\{\,e\,\right\}
, which by Definition
F.7
is a typing interpretation w.r.t.
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta
and
B_{s}

if
the transition

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{B_{s}}\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v

can be concluded by the typed semantics for some state
\text{env}_{SV}
built according to Definition
3.2
, and which by Lemma
3.3
therefore is ensured to be well-typed and consistent w.r.t.
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta
.

Furthermore, from
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash e:B_{s}
, we know that all variable names, addresses and field names in
e
must exist in
\Gamma
and
\Delta
; so, by the construction of
\text{env}_{SV}
, we therefore also know that

•

\mathcal{F}_{A}(e)\subseteq\dom\left(\text{env}_{S}\right)
, and

•

\mathcal{F}_{V}(e)\subseteq\dom\left(\text{env}_{V}\right)
.

The statement to be shown then simplifies to

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash e:B_{s}\implies\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{B_{s}}\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v

We show this by induction on the inference for
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash e:B_{s}
:
There are two base cases:

•

Suppose the type judgment was concluded by
; then,
e=v
. From the premise and the side conditions, we know that

\displaystyle\text{{TypeOf}}_{\Gamma}(v)

\displaystyle=(B^{\prime},s^{\prime})

\displaystyle s^{\prime}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq s

\displaystyle\Sigma

\displaystyle\vdash B^{\prime}\mathrel{<:}B

This satisfies the side conditions of
, so we can infer that

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{B_{s}}\left<v,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v

•

Suppose the type judgment was concluded by
; then,
e=x
. From the premise and side conditions, we know that

\displaystyle\Delta(x)

\displaystyle=\text{var}(B_{2},s_{2})

\displaystyle s_{2}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq s

\displaystyle\Sigma

\displaystyle\vdash B_{2}\mathrel{<:}B

By assumption,
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash\text{env}_{V}
, which was concluded by
.
We know
x\in\dom\left(\text{env}_{V}\right)
, so
\text{env}_{V}(x)=v
by the assumption
\mathcal{F}_{V}(e)\subseteq\dom\left(\text{env}_{V}\right)
.
Therefore, let
\text{env}_{V}=\text{env}_{V}^{\prime},(x,v)
.

From the premise and side condition of the aforementioned rule, we then know that

\displaystyle\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta

\displaystyle\vdash v:(B_{2},s_{2})

\displaystyle\Delta(x)

\displaystyle=\text{var}(B_{2},s_{2})

Now,
\Gamma;\Delta\vdash v:(B_{2},s_{2})
must have been concluded by
, and from its side condition we know that

\displaystyle\text{{TypeOf}}_{\Gamma}(v)

\displaystyle=(B_{1},s_{1})

\displaystyle s_{1}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq s_{2}

\displaystyle\Sigma

\displaystyle\vdash B_{1}\mathrel{<:}B_{2}

This satisfies all the side conditions of
, so we can infer that

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{B_{s}}\left<x,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v

There are two inductive cases:

•

Suppose the type judgment was concluded by
; then,
e=e^{\prime}.p
. From the premise and side conditions, we know that

\displaystyle\Gamma;\Delta

\displaystyle\vdash e^{\prime}:I_{s}

\displaystyle\Gamma(I)(p)

\displaystyle=\text{var}(B_{2},s_{2})

\displaystyle s_{2}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq s

\displaystyle\Sigma

\displaystyle\vdash B_{2}\mathrel{<:}B

By the induction hypothesis on
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash e^{\prime}:I_{s}
, we obtain that

\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{I_{s}}\left<e^{\prime},\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow X

for some address
X
, since only addresses are given a type
I
by the definition of
\text{{TypeOf}}_{\Gamma}(\cdot)
(Definition
2.4
).

By assumption,
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash\text{env}_{S}
holds, which was concluded by
.
We know that
X\in\dom\left(\text{env}_{S}\right)
; so,
\text{env}_{S}(X)=\text{env}_{F}
because of
\mathcal{F}_{A}(e)\subseteq\dom\left(\text{env}_{S}\right)
and consistency.
Therefore, let
\text{env}_{S}=\text{env}_{S}^{\prime},X:\text{env}_{F}
.
From the premises and side conditions of this rule, we know that
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash_{X}\text{env}_{F}
, which must have been concluded by
.
We know that
p\in\dom\left(\text{env}_{F}\right)
, so
\text{env}_{F}(p)=v
by consistency and well-typedness of
\text{env}_{SV}
.
This establishes that
\text{env}_{S}(X)(p)=v
.

Now, let
\text{env}_{F}=\text{env}_{F}^{\prime},p:v
.
From the premises and side conditions of
, we know that

6
6
footnotetext:
In principle, instead of
\Gamma(X)=(I,s)
, we could have
\Gamma(X)=(I^{\prime},s^{\prime})
. However, by the coercion property (Theorem
F.2
), we then have that
\Sigma\vdash I^{\prime}\mathrel{<:}I
and
s^{\prime}\sqsubseteq s
, so we omit this extra complication.

\displaystyle\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta

\displaystyle\vdash v:(B_{1},s_{1})

\displaystyle\Gamma(X)

\displaystyle=(I,s)

\displaystyle\Gamma(I)(p)

\displaystyle=\text{var}(B_{2},s_{2})

Judgement
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash v:(B_{1},s_{1})
must have been concluded by
, and from its side condition we know that

\displaystyle\text{{TypeOf}}_{\Gamma}(v)

\displaystyle=(B_{1},s_{1})

\displaystyle s_{1}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq s_{2}

\displaystyle\Sigma

\displaystyle\vdash B_{1}\mathrel{<:}B_{2}

This satisfies all the side conditions of
, so we can infer that

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{B_{s}}\left<e.p,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v

•

Suppose the type judgment was concluded by
;
then,
e=\op(\widetilde{e})
. From the premises, we know that

\displaystyle\vdash\op:\widetilde{B}\to B

\displaystyle\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta

\displaystyle\vdash\widetilde{e}:\widetilde{B}_{\widetilde{s}}

For each
e_{i}
in
\widetilde{e}
and
(B_{i},s)
, we can use the induction hypothesis to obtain that

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{(B_{i},s)}\left<e_{i},\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v_{i}

Now, by the safety requirement for operations (Definition
C.1
), we know that it must be the case that
\op(\widetilde{v})\rightarrow_{\op}v
and
\text{{TypeOf}}_{\Gamma}(v)=(B,s_{\bot})
.
Furthermore,
s_{\bot}\sqsubseteq s
holds by definition of
s_{\bot}
.
Thus, this satisfies all the premises and side conditions of
, so we can infer

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{B_{s}}\left<\op(\widetilde{e}),\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v\qed

Combining Theorems
F.1
,
F.8
, and
F.3
gives us the equivalent of the soundness theorem for expressions from
[
2
]
, which now follows as a simple corollary:

Corollary F.9 (Static soundness for expressions).

If

•

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash e:B_{s}
, and

•

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash\text{env}_{SV}
, and

•

\Gamma\vdash\text{env}_{SV}
, and

•

\mathcal{F}_{A}(e)\subseteq\dom\left(\text{env}_{S}\right)
, and

•

\mathcal{F}_{V}(e)\subseteq\dom\left(\text{env}_{V}\right)
,

then there exists some
v
such that
\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v
, and
\text{{TypeOf}}_{\Gamma}(v)=(B_{1},s_{1})
for some
B_{1}
and
s_{1}
such that
\Sigma\vdash B_{1}\mathrel{<:}B
and
s_{1}\sqsubseteq s
, and every container, that will be read from in the evaluation of
e
, is of type
\text{var}(B_{2},s_{2})
, for some
B_{2}
, such that
s_{2}\sqsubseteq s
.

Proof.

By Theorem
F.8
, we have that

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash e:B_{s}\implies\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash e:B_{s}

Unfolding the definition of
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash e:B_{s}
(Definition
F.7
) yields that there must exist a typing interpretation
\mathcal{R}^{B_{s}}_{\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta}
containing
e
, which again means that the transition

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{B_{s}}\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v

can be concluded by the typed semantics for any consistent and well-typed
\text{env}_{SV}
containing at least all the names used in
e
.
By Theorem
F.3
, this transition implies that
\text{{TypeOf}}_{\Gamma}(v)=(B_{1},s_{1})
and
s_{1}\sqsubseteq s
and every container read from in
e
is of level
s
or lower.
Finally, by Theorem
F.1
, we have that

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{B_{s}}\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v\implies\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow v\qed

Finally, we can also obtain the equivalent of the non-interference theorem for expressions from
[
2
]
as a simple corollary of Corollaries
F.4
and
F.9
:

Corollary F.10 (Static non-interference for expressions).

If

•

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash e:B_{s}
, and

•

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash\text{env}_{SV}^{1}
,
\Gamma\vdash\text{env}_{SV}^{1}
,
\mathcal{F}_{A}(e)\subseteq\dom\left(\text{env}_{S}^{1}\right)
,
\mathcal{F}_{V}(e)\subseteq\dom\left(\text{env}_{V}^{1}\right)
, and

•

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash\text{env}_{SV}^{2}
,
\Gamma\vdash\text{env}_{SV}^{2}
,
\mathcal{F}_{A}(e)\subseteq\dom\left(\text{env}_{S}^{2}\right)
,
\mathcal{F}_{V}(e)\subseteq\dom\left(\text{env}_{V}^{2}\right)
, and

•

\Gamma;\Delta\vdash\text{env}_{SV}^{1}=_{s}\text{env}_{SV}^{2}
,

then
\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}^{1}\right>\rightarrow v
and
\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}^{2}\right>\rightarrow v
.

Proof.

From the assumptions, and by two applications of Corollary
F.9
, we get that

\displaystyle\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}^{1}\right>

\displaystyle\rightarrow v_{1}

\displaystyle\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}^{2}\right>

\displaystyle\rightarrow v_{2}

such that no container of a level higher than
s
was read from
\text{env}_{SV}^{1}
resp.
\text{env}_{SV}^{2}
in the two evaluations of
e
.
By assumption,
\Gamma;\Delta\vdash\text{env}_{SV}^{1}=_{s}\text{env}_{SV}^{2}
, so the two states agree on all entries of level
s
or lower.
Thus, the two evaluations of
e
will use the same values, and will therefore yield the same result, so
v_{1}=v_{2}
.
∎

With this last result, we have recovered the properties ensured by syntactic well-typedness for expressions in
[
2
]
, but through a quite different route; namely by starting from a semantics of types for expressions, given in terms of a typed semantics, that is compatible with the untyped semantics and ensures that the restrictions imposed by the type predicates are in fact observed.

Appendix G
Well-formedness of stacks

The notion of
well-formedness
of stacks w.r.t. type environments
\Sigma;\Gamma
, a state
\text{env}_{SV}
, and a security level
s
, is defined by the rules in Figure
24
.

[
wf-bot
]

\dfrac{\phantom{P}}{\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{SV};s\vdash\bot}\par

[
wf-stm
]

\dfrac{\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{SV};s\vdash Q}{\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{SV};s\vdash S;Q}\;\left(\begin{array}[]{r @{~} l}\mathcal{F}_{A}(S)&\subseteq\dom\left(\text{env}_{S}\right)\\
\mathcal{F}_{V}(S)&\subseteq\dom\left(\text{env}_{V}\right)\end{array}\right)\par

[
wf-del
]

\dfrac{\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{SV};s\vdash Q}{\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{SV},x:v;s\vdash\text{del}(x);Q}\par

[
wf-ret
]

\dfrac{\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash\text{env}_{V}^{\prime}\quad\text{env}_{S}\vdash\text{env}_{V}^{\prime}\quad\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{S};\text{env}_{V}^{\prime};s\vdash Q}{\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{S};\text{env}_{V};s\vdash(\text{env}_{V}^{\prime},\Delta);Q}\par

[
wf-sec
]

\dfrac{\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{SV};s^{\prime}\vdash Q}{\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{SV};s\vdash s^{\prime};Q}\;\left(s\sqsupseteq s^{\prime}\right)

Figure 24.
Well-formedness of stacks
Q
.

In
we require that any statement
S
found on the stack must only contain names found in the domains of
\text{env}_{SV}
.
Together with consistency of
\text{env}_{SV}
, this ensures that transitions cannot be stuck due to a free name, or a missing contract implementation.
In
, we remove
x
from
\text{env}_{V}
when we encounter an end-of-scope symbol
\text{del}(x)
, since the domain of
\text{env}_{V}
is used in the rule
.
The rule
handles the of a return symbol
(\text{env}_{V}^{\prime},\Delta)
occurring on the stack.
In this case, we must here require that
\text{env}_{V}
is both well-typed and consistent, and the remainder of the stack must then be well-formed relative to this new
\text{env}_{V}^{\prime}
.
Finally, rule
ensures that all security levels
s^{\prime}
occurring in the stack are in descending order.

Well-formedness ensures that
Q
and
\text{env}_{SV}
can meaningfully be combined in a configuration
\left<Q,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>
and executed by the typed semantics at level
s
, provided that the initial state
\text{env}_{SV}
is well-typed, and
\text{env}_{TSV}
are consistent, and that
s
is greater than, or equal to, the first security level occurring in
Q
.
Note that it is necessary to assume consistency of
\text{env}_{T}
as well to ensure that well-formedness of
Q
is preserved, since a transition can place code from
\text{env}_{T}
on the stack.

Appendix H
Typed Operational Semantics

As was the case with the typed semantics of expressions, the rules for the typed semantics of stacks are obtained from the rules for the untyped semantics, but with added restrictions from the type rules for statements and stacks.
Every transition that can be concluded by the typed semantics can therefore also be concluded by the untyped semantics, with one exception: the transitions
which restore a security level
s
that had previously been placed on the stack.
There is nothing similar in the untyped semantics.Hence, to show a statement corresponding to Theorem
F.1
for stacks, we first need to remove all security levels
s
, and also merge the type information from
\Delta
into variable environments
\text{env}_{V}
placed on the stack.
This is done by functions
\text{{rem}}(Q)
and
\text{{mrg}}(\text{env}_{V};\Delta)
, defined on the syntax of return symbols and stacks, respectively:

\displaystyle\text{{mrg}}(\text{env}_{V}^{\emptyset};\emptyset)

\displaystyle=\text{env}_{V}^{\emptyset}

\displaystyle\text{{mrg}}(x:v,\text{env}_{V};x:B_{s},\Delta)

\displaystyle=(x,v,B_{s}),\text{{mrg}}(\text{env}_{V},\Delta)

\displaystyle\text{{rem}}(\bot)

\displaystyle=\bot

\displaystyle\text{{rem}}(S;Q)

\displaystyle=S;\text{{rem}}(Q)

\displaystyle\text{{rem}}(\text{del}(x);Q)

\displaystyle=\text{del}(x);\text{{rem}}(Q)

\displaystyle\text{{rem}}((\text{env}_{V},\Delta);Q)

\displaystyle=\text{{mrg}}(\text{env}_{V},\Delta);\text{{rem}}(Q)

\displaystyle\text{{rem}}(s;Q)

\displaystyle=\text{{rem}}(Q)

It will also be useful to know the security lever of the first symbol in a stack:

\displaystyle\text{{fst}}(\bot)

\displaystyle=s_{\bot}

\displaystyle\text{{fst}}(S;Q)

\displaystyle=\text{{fst}}(Q)

\displaystyle\text{{fst}}(\text{del}(x);Q)

\displaystyle=\text{{fst}}(Q)

\displaystyle\text{{fst}}((\text{env}_{V},\Delta);Q)

\displaystyle=\text{{fst}}(Q)

\displaystyle\text{{fst}}(s;Q)

\displaystyle=s

Notice that the last function returns
s_{\bot}
for the bottom of the stack.
This corresponds to no restriction on the level at which the stack can be executed, so in that case we can choose the execution level arbitrarily low.

Theorem H.1 (Semantic compatibility for stacks).

Let all security levels in
Q
occur in descending order.
For all type environments
\Sigma
,
\Gamma
,
\Delta
, and security level
s\sqsupseteq\text{{fst}}(Q)
, and environments
\text{env}_{TSV}
, it holds that either

(1)

\begin{split}&\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s}\left<Q,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>\rightarrow\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta^{\prime}\vDash_{s^{\prime}}\left<Q^{\prime},\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime}\right>\\
\implies&\left<\text{{rem}}(Q),\text{env}_{TS};\text{{mrg}}(\text{env}_{V};\Delta)\right>\rightarrow\left<\text{{rem}}(Q),\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{S}^{\prime};\text{{mrg}}(\text{env}_{V}^{\prime};\Delta^{\prime})\right>\end{split}

or otherwise there exists a sequence of transitions

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s}\left<Q,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>\rightarrow\ldots\rightarrow\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s^{\prime}}\left<Q^{\prime},\text{env}_{TSV}\right>

of finite length, to a state for which the implication in (
1
) holds.

Proof.

Firstly, suppose
Q=s^{\prime};Q^{\prime}
.
Then
\text{{fst}}(Q)=s^{\prime}
and
s\sqsupseteq s^{\prime}
by assumption.
Thus

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s}\left<s^{\prime};Q^{\prime},\text{env}_{TSV}\right>\rightarrow\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s^{\prime}}\left<Q^{\prime},\text{env}_{TSV}\right>

can then be concluded by
.
It might now again be the case that
Q^{\prime}=s^{\prime\prime};Q^{\prime\prime}
, but then
s^{\prime}\sqsupseteq s^{\prime\prime}
by the assumption of ordering of the security levels.
Hence, the transition can again be concluded by
, and so on.
As we know the stack is of finite length, it will eventually be the case that the symbol at the top of the stack is
not
a security level, in which case the theorem holds if the statement in (
1
) holds.

When
Q\neq s^{\prime};Q^{\prime}
, we proceed by case analysis of the rules used to conclude the transition

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s}\left<Q,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>\rightarrow\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta^{\prime}\vDash_{s^{\prime}}\left<Q^{\prime},\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime}\right>

In each case, we obtain the required premises and side conditions needed to conclude the same transition in the untyped semantics from the rule of the typed semantics.
This is straightforward, since each typed semantic rule (with the exception of
) has an untyped variant, whose premises and side conditions are a subset of the premises and side conditions of the typed variant.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

By case analysis of the rules used to conclude the transition.

•

For rules
,
,
,
, and
, the conclusion follows directly from the fact that these rules do not impose any restrictions on the security level.

•

For rules
,
,
,
,
,
, and
, we have a side condition of the form
s_{1}\sqsubseteq s^{\prime}
restricting the security level, where
s^{\prime}
is derived from the premise of the rule.
As we know that
s_{2}\sqsubseteq s_{1}
, we therefore also have that
s_{2}\sqsubseteq s^{\prime}
by transitivity of
\sqsubseteq
, which, for each rule, allows us to conclude the transition with
s_{2}
as well.

•

Finally, if
was used to conclude the transition, we have in the side condition that
s_{1}\sqsupseteq s^{\prime}
, where
s^{\prime}
is the top element of the stack
Q
.
Hence,
\text{{fst}}(Q)=s^{\prime}
, and by assumption
s_{2}\sqsupseteq\text{{fst}}(Q)
so
s_{2}\sqsupseteq s^{\prime}
.
Thus we can satisfy the side condition with
s_{2}
as well and conclude the transition with
. ∎

Proof of Lemma
3.3
.

The construction of Definition
3.2
is a kind of “reverse reading” of the well-typedness and consistency rules for
\text{env}_{SV}
(see Figures
17
and
22
).
The proofs of well-typedness and consistency are then obtained through an easy induction on the inferences of the respective judgements.
∎

Proof of Corollary
3.4
.

By case analysis on the rules of the typed semantics (Figures
2
and
3.1
).
The environments
\text{env}_{SV}
only affect the execution of a statement through variable and field names appearing in expressions, and for these cases we apply Theorem
F.6
for the evaluation of all expressions occurring inside statements in
Q
.
∎

Finally, we can show a result concerning the type environment
\Delta
and the security level
s
.
Both may have changed after a transition, in case the security level was raised, a local variable was declared, or a method call was issued.
However, any such change is ‘temporary’, in the sense that all of them have resulted in a special symbol pushed onto the stack, which undoes the change whenever reached.
Thus,
if
a stack
Q
terminates normally (i.e., by reaching the bottom symbol
\bot
), then all such temporary changes have been discarded.
Assuming
Q
is executed relative to some
\Delta
and
s
, we can detect the type environment and security level
(\Delta^{\prime},s^{\prime})
on which
Q
will terminate,
if
it terminates, as follows. This will be used in the proofs of Appendix
I
.

Definition H.2 (Terminal
\Delta
and
s
).

We define the function
\text{{fin}}(\cdot)
on triplets
(Q,\Delta,s)
of stacks
Q
, type environments
\Delta
and security levels
s
as follows:

\displaystyle\text{{fin}}(\bot,\Delta,s)

\displaystyle=(\Delta,s)

\displaystyle\text{{fin}}(S;Q,\Delta,s)

\displaystyle=\text{{fin}}(Q,\Delta,s)

\displaystyle\text{{fin}}(\text{del}(x);Q,(\Delta,x:\text{var}(B_{s^{\prime}})),s)

\displaystyle=\text{{fin}}(Q,\Delta,s)

\displaystyle\text{{fin}}((\text{env}_{V},\Delta^{\prime});Q,\Delta,s)

\displaystyle=\text{{fin}}(Q,\Delta^{\prime},s)

\displaystyle\text{{fin}}(s^{\prime};Q,\Delta,s)

\displaystyle=\text{{fin}}(Q,\Delta,s^{\prime})

Theorem H.3 (Terminal type level).

If
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s}\left<Q,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>\rightarrow^{*}\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta^{\prime}\vDash_{s^{\prime}}\left<\bot,\text{env}_{T},\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime}\right>
, then
(\Delta^{\prime},s^{\prime})=\text{{fin}}(Q,\Delta,s)
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on the length of
\rightarrow^{*}
.
In the base case,
Q=\bot
,
\Delta^{\prime}=\Delta
and
s^{\prime}=s
, and it trivially holds by definition of
\text{{fin}}(\cdot)
.
For the inductive case, let

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s}\left<Q,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>\rightarrow\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta^{\prime\prime}\vDash_{s^{\prime\prime}}\left<Q^{\prime\prime},\text{env}_{T},\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime\prime}\right>\rightarrow^{*}\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta^{\prime}\vDash_{s^{\prime}}\left<Q^{\prime},\text{env}_{T},\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime}\right>

and let us proceed by case analysis of the semantic rule used to conclude the first transition:

•

The cases for
,
,
,
are immediately seen to hold, since neither
\Delta
nor
s
are modified.

•

The cases for
and
are similar, so we shall only consider the case for
.
We have that
Q=\texttt{if $e$ then $S_{\text{T}}$ else $S_{\text{F}}$};\hat{Q}
and
Q^{\prime\prime}=S_{b};s;\hat{Q}
.
By induction and Definition
H.2
, we can conclude

(\Delta^{\prime},s^{\prime})=\text{{fin}}(S;s;\hat{Q},\Delta,s^{\prime\prime})=\text{{fin}}(s;\hat{Q},\Delta,s^{\prime\prime})=\text{{fin}}(\hat{Q},\Delta,s)=\text{{fin}}(Q,\Delta,s)

•

If
was used, then
Q=\texttt{$\text{var}(B_{\bar{s}})$ $x$ := $e$ in $S$};\hat{Q}
and
Q^{\prime\prime}=S;\text{del}(x);\hat{Q}
; moreover,

\Delta^{\prime\prime}=\Delta,x:\text{var}(B_{\bar{s}})
and
s^{\prime\prime}=s
. By induction and Definition
H.2
, we can conclude

\displaystyle(\Delta^{\prime},s^{\prime})=~

\displaystyle\text{{fin}}(S;\text{del}(x);\hat{Q},(\Delta,x:\text{var}(B_{\bar{s}})),s)

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\text{{fin}}(\text{del}(x);\hat{Q},(\Delta,x:\text{var}(B_{\bar{s}})),s)

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\text{{fin}}(\hat{Q},\Delta,s)

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\text{{fin}}(Q,\Delta,s)

•

The cases for
,
and
are similar, since the reduct has the same form in all three cases.
We shall only consider the case for
, where
we have that
Q=\texttt{call $e_{1}$.$m$($\widetilde{e}$)\$$e_{2}$};\hat{Q}
and
Q^{\prime\prime}=S;(\text{env}_{V},\Delta);s;\hat{Q}
.
By induction and Definition
H.2
,

\displaystyle(\Delta^{\prime},s^{\prime})=~

\displaystyle\text{{fin}}(S;(\text{env}_{V},\Delta);s;\hat{Q},\Delta^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\text{{fin}}((\text{env}_{V},\Delta);s;\hat{Q},\Delta^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\text{{fin}}(s;\hat{Q},\Delta,s^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\text{{fin}}(\hat{Q},\Delta,s)

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\text{{fin}}(Q,\Delta,s)

•

If
was used, then
Q=\text{del}(x);Q^{\prime\prime}
,
\Delta=\Delta^{\prime\prime},x:\text{var}(B_{\bar{s}})
and
s^{\prime\prime}=s
. Hence, by induction and Definition
H.2
, we have

(\Delta^{\prime},s^{\prime})=\text{{fin}}(Q^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime})=\text{{fin}}(Q,\Delta,s)

•

If
was used, then
Q=(\text{env}_{V}^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{\prime\prime});Q^{\prime\prime}
and
s^{\prime\prime}=s
.
Like before, by induction and Definition
H.2
, we have

(\Delta^{\prime},s^{\prime})=\text{{fin}}(Q^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime})=\text{{fin}}(Q,\Delta,s)

•

If
was used, then
Q=s^{\prime\prime};Q^{\prime\prime}
and
\Delta^{\prime\prime}=\Delta
.
Again by induction and Definition

,

(\Delta^{\prime},s^{\prime})=\text{{fin}}(Q^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime})=\text{{fin}}(Q,\Delta,s)\qed

We now provide a few auxiliary lemmas for the proof of Theorem
3.5
.
The lemmas are shown by induction on the length of the type inference (Figure
17
), or of the consistency inference (Figure
22
), or of the well-formedness inference (Figure
24
).
In the substitution lemmas (Lemmas
H.6
–
H.7
) we incorporate consistency in addition to well-typedness.
They could equally well be shown separately, but since we shall always require both properties to hold for the
\text{env}_{SV}
we shall consider, we prefer to combine them to avoid repeating essentially the same premises.

Lemma H.4 (Weakening of
\Delta
).

If
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash\text{env}_{V}
, and
x\notin\dom\left(\text{env}_{V}\right)
, then
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta,x:T\vdash\text{env}_{V}
.

Lemma H.5 (Strengthening of
\Delta
).

If
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta,x:T\vdash\text{env}_{V}
, and
x\notin\dom\left(\text{env}_{V}\right)
, then
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vdash\text{env}_{V}
.

Lemma H.6 (Substitution in
\text{env}_{V}
).

Assume that

•

\Gamma\vdash\text{env}_{SV}
(consistency)

•

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta,x:\text{var}(B_{s})\vdash\text{env}_{V}
(well-typedness)

•

x\in\dom\left(\text{env}_{V}\right)

•

if
\Sigma;\Gamma;\emptyset\vdash v:B_{s}
and
v\in\mathcal{N}_{A}
, then
v\in\dom\left(\text{env}_{S}\right)
.

Then,
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta,x:\text{var}(B_{s})\vdash\text{env}_{V}^{\prime}
and
\Gamma\vdash\text{env}_{S},\text{env}_{V}^{\prime}
, where
\text{env}_{V}^{\prime}=\text{env}_{V}\kern-2.0pt\left[x\mapsto v\right]
.

Lemma H.7 (Substitution in
\text{env}_{S}
).

Assume that

•

\Gamma;\text{env}_{S}\vdash\text{env}_{S}
(consistency)

•

\Sigma;\Gamma;\emptyset\vdash\text{env}_{S}
(well-typedness)

•

X\in\dom\left(\text{env}_{S}\right)

•

\Gamma(X)=(I,s^{\prime\prime})

•

\Gamma(I)(p)=\text{var}(B_{s})

•

if
\Sigma;\Gamma;\emptyset\vdash v:(B_{s})
and
v\in\mathcal{N}_{A}
, then
v\in\dom\left(\text{env}_{S}\right)
.

Then,
\Sigma;\Gamma;\emptyset\vdash\text{env}_{S}^{\prime}
and
\Gamma;\text{env}_{S}^{\prime}\vdash\text{env}_{S}^{\prime}
, where
\text{env}_{S}^{\prime}=\text{env}_{S}\kern-2.0pt\left[X\mapsto\text{env}_{F}\kern-2.0pt\left[p\mapsto v\right]\right]
.

Lemma H.8 (Properties of well-formedness).

•

If
\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{SV};s\vdash Q
then
s\sqsupseteq\text{{fst}}(Q)
.

•

If
\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{SV};s\vdash Q
and
s^{\prime}\sqsupseteq s
then
\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{SV};s^{\prime}\vdash s;Q
.

•

If
\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{SV};s\vdash Q
and
x\in\dom\left(\text{env}_{V}\right)
then
\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{S},\text{env}_{V}\kern-2.0pt\left[x\mapsto v\right];s\vdash Q
for any
v
.

•

If
\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{SV};s\vdash Q
and
X\in\dom\left(\text{env}_{S}\right)
and
p\in\dom\left(\text{env}_{S}(X)\right)
then
\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{S}\kern-2.0pt\left[X\mapsto\text{env}_{F}\kern-2.0pt\left[p\mapsto v\right]\right],\text{env}_{V};s\vdash Q
for any
v
.

.

By case analysis of the rules used to conclude the transition (Figs.

–

Proof of Theorem
3.6
.

We first consider the two field environments.
We know that

\Gamma\vdash\text{env}_{S}^{1}=_{s}\text{env}_{S}^{2}.

By two applications of Theorem
3.5
, we have that, for all
s^{\prime\prime}
such that
s\not\sqsubseteq s^{\prime\prime}
:

Γ⊢
\text{env}_{S}
^1 =_s”
\text{env}_{S}
^1’
Γ⊢
\text{env}_{S}
^2 =_s”
\text{env}_{S}
^2’

So, both environments agree before and after the transition on all fields that are
strictly lower
than, or unrelated to,
s
.
Hence, we also have that

\forall s^{\prime\prime}\;.\;s\not\sqsubseteq s^{\prime\prime}\implies\Gamma\vdash\text{env}_{S}^{1^{\prime}}=_{s^{\prime\prime}}\text{env}_{S}^{2^{\prime}}

Suppose now that the two transitions modified a field that is exactly at level
s
(the modified field must be the same in both transitions, since
Q
is the same).
From the semantics (Figures
2
–
3.1
) we see that there are only three rules that could have been used to conclude a transition that modifies
\text{env}_{S}
:
,
and
.
The two call rules can modify the
balance
fields of the caller and callee, and the assignment rule can modify any other field.
We consider only
, but the argument is the same also for the two other rules.
The transitions are of the form

\begin{split}&\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s}\left<\texttt{this.$p$ := $e$};Q,\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{i}\right>\\
\rightarrow~&\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s}\left<Q,\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{S}^{i}\kern-2.0pt\left[X\mapsto\text{env}_{F}\kern-2.0pt\left[p\mapsto v_{i}\right]\right];\text{env}_{V}^{i}\right>\end{split}

for
i\in\left\{\,1,2\,\right\}
, and from the premise of that rule we have that

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{(B,s^{\prime})}\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}^{i}\right>\rightarrow v_{i}.

By Corollary
F.4
, if

Σ; Γ; Δ
\vDash_{(B,s^{\prime})}

\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}^{1}\right>

\rightarrow
v_1
Σ; Γ; Δ
\vDash_{(B,s^{\prime})}

\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}^{2}\right>

\rightarrow
v_2

then
v_{1}=v_{2}
; so, the expression in the premise yields the same value
v
in both executions.
Hence, the field
p
at level
s
may be changed in the two executions, but it will be changed to the
same value
.
Thus
\text{env}_{S}^{1^{\prime}}
and
\text{env}_{S}^{2^{\prime}}
also agree at level
s
.

For
\text{env}_{V}^{i}
, we do not have a result similar to Theorem
3.5
, so we must examine all the rules that could have been used to conclude the transition:

•

If one of the rules
,
,
,
,
, or
was used, then the result is immediate, as the transition does not modify
\text{env}_{V}^{i}
.

•

If
was used, the transitions are of the form

\begin{split}&\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta,x:\text{var}(B_{s^{\prime}})\vDash_{s}\left<\text{del}(x);Q,\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{S}^{i};\text{env}_{V}^{i},x:v_{i}\right>\\
\rightarrow~&\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s}\left<Q,\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{S}^{i};\text{env}_{V}^{i}\right>\end{split}

So, for both
\text{env}_{V}^{1}
and
\text{env}_{V}^{2}
we are removing the entry for the variable
x
.
Equality of the remaining entries, for level
s
and lower, then follows from the initial assumption of
s
-equality of the environments.

•

If
was used, the transitions are of the form

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s}\left<(\text{env}_{V}^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime});Q,\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{i}\right>\rightarrow\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta^{\prime}\vDash_{s}\left<Q,\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{S}^{i};\text{env}_{V}^{\prime}\right>

i.e. the same variable environment
\text{env}_{V}^{\prime}
is taken off the stack in both transitions, so they obviously agree since they are equal.

•

If one of
or
was used, then the transition either extends the variable environment with a new entry, or modifies an existing entry.
In either case, the argument is essentially the same.
In the premise, we have an expression evaluation of the form

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{B_{s^{\prime}}}\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}^{i}\right>\rightarrow v_{i}.

where
s^{\prime}
is the level of the variable being assigned to.
There are now two cases:

(1)

If
s^{\prime}\not\sqsubseteq s
, i.e.
s^{\prime}
is
strictly higher
than
s
, (or, in the case of
, unrelated to
s
), then the result is immediate, since only variables of level
s
or lower must agree in the two environments for the result to hold.

(2)

If
s^{\prime}\sqsubseteq s
, we have by Corollary
F.4
that if

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{B_{s^{\prime}}}\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}^{1}\right>\rightarrow v_{1}\qquad\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{B_{s^{\prime}}}\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}^{2}\right>\rightarrow v_{2}

then
v_{1}=v_{2}
; so, the expression in the premise yields the same value
v
in both executions.
Hence, the two environments will contain the same value
v
for the variable
x
.
Equality for all variables of level
s
or lower then follows from the initial assumption of
s
-equality of the two environments.

•

Finally, if one of the call rules
,
, or
was used, then the

environments will be two

environments created in the transition, to contain the bindings for the formal parameters

of the method call and for the magic variables.

For each variable binding, the argument is then exactly the same as for
and
above:
Either the variable is of a level strictly higher than, or unrelated to,

, in which case its value is allowed to differ in the two executions; or the variable is of level

or lower, in which case the corresponding expression also must be evaluated at level
s
or lower, and then we obtain the same value
v
in both executions by Corollary
F.4
.
Thus,
s
-equality of the two environments is again ensured. ∎

Appendix I
Semantic typing of stacks and commands

Here we provide the missing details and proofs for the results in Section
3.3
.

Proof of Lemma
3.9
.

By induction on the length of
\Mapsto^{*}
. The base case is an immediate consequence of Definition
3.8
. For the inductive step, let us consider
\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{T}\vDash(Q,\Delta,s)\Mapsto(Q^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime})\Mapsto^{*}(Q^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime},s^{\prime})
; by induction, we know that
(Q^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime})\not\in\nu\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
and so, again by Definition
3.8
, it cannot be that
(Q,\Delta,s)\in\nu\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
.
∎

In some of the following proofs, we shall need to concatenate two stacks, that is, take a stack
Q_{1}
, remove the bottom symbol
\bot
, and then append the remainder on top of another stack
Q_{2}
. Thus, we write
q
for a finite sequence of stack symbols
except

\bot
;
i.e.

\begin{array}[]{r @{~} c @{~} l @{\qquad} l}q&::=&\epsilon~~\big|~~~S;q~~\big|~~~\text{del}(x);q~~\big|~~~(\text{env}_{V},\Delta);q~~\big|~~~s;q\end{array}

So, we can alternatively write a stack
Q
as
q;\bot
.

In the following proofs, we shall frequently use function
\text{{fst}}(\cdot)
, defined in Definition
H.2
.

Proof of Lemma
3.10
.

By coinduction.
From
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash Q:\text{cmd}(s_{1})
we know there exists a typing interpretation
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
containing
(Q,\Delta,s_{1})
.
We create a candidate typing interpretation
\mathcal{R}
containing
(Q,\Delta,s_{2})
thus:

\mathcal{R}\triangleq\left\{\,(Q^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime},s_{2}^{\prime}{\\
}where:{\\
}(&~Q^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime},s_{1}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}\land s_{1}^{\prime}\sqsupseteq s_{2}^{\prime}\sqsupseteq\text{{fst}}(Q^{\prime})\,\right\}

and clearly, since
(Q,\Delta,s_{1})\in\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
and
s_{1}\sqsupseteq s_{2}\sqsupseteq\text{{fst}}(Q)
, we also have that
(Q,\Delta,s_{2})\in\mathcal{R}
.

We must now show that
\mathcal{R}
indeed is a typing interpretation according to Definition
3.8
.
Pick any
(Q^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime},s_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}
;
there are now two cases:

(1)

If
Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{TQ}
, then it satisfies Case
1
of Definition
3.8
.

(2)

Otherwise, we know that
(Q^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime},s_{1}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
and, for all appropriately shaped
\text{env}_{SV}
, there exists at least one transition

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta^{\prime}\vDash_{s_{1}^{\prime}}\left<Q^{\prime},\text{env}_{TSV}\right>\rightarrow\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta^{\prime\prime}\vDash_{s_{1}^{\prime\prime}}\left<Q^{\prime\prime},\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime}\right>

such that
(Q^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{\prime\prime},s_{1}^{\prime\prime})\in\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
. By Proposition
3.1
, we then have that the transition

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta^{\prime}\vDash_{s_{2}^{\prime}}\left<Q^{\prime},\text{env}_{TSV}\right>\rightarrow\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta^{\prime\prime}\vDash_{s_{2}^{\prime\prime}}\left<Q^{\prime\prime},\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime}\right>

can be concluded for any
s_{2}^{\prime}
such that
s_{1}^{\prime}\sqsupseteq s_{2}^{\prime}\sqsupseteq\text{{fst}}(Q^{\prime})
, and by construction of
\mathcal{R}
, we also have that
(Q^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{\prime\prime},s_{2}^{\prime\prime})\in\mathcal{R}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.13
.

By the definition of
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash S;Q:\text{cmd}(s)
, we must exhibit a typing interpretation
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
containing the triplet
(S;Q,\Delta,s)
.

From
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash S:\text{cmd}(s)
and
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash Q:\text{cmd}(s)
we know there exist typing interpretations
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{2}
such that

\displaystyle(S;\bot,\Delta,s)

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{1}

\displaystyle(Q,\Delta,s)

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{2}

We then construct the following candidate typing interpretation:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{\,(q;Q,\Delta^{\prime},s^{\prime})\mid(q;\bot,\Delta^{\prime},s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{1}\land\text{{fin}}(q;\bot,\Delta^{\prime},s^{\prime})=(\Delta,s)\,\right\}

\displaystyle\cup\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{2}

where
q
may be empty.
As
(S;\bot,\Delta,s)\in\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{1}
and
\text{{fin}}(S;\bot,\Delta,s)=(\Delta,s)
, we have by construction that
(S;Q,\Delta,s)\in\mathcal{R}
.

Now we must show that
\mathcal{R}
indeed is a typing interpretation.
Consider an arbitrary triplet
(Q_{1},\Delta_{1},s_{1})\in\mathcal{R}
.
If
Q_{1}\in\mathcal{TQ}
, then Case
1
of Definition
3.8
holds.
Otherwise, there are three possibilities:

•

If
Q_{1}
is of the form
q_{1};Q
for non-empty
q_{1}
, then
(q_{1};\bot,\Delta_{1},s_{1})\in\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{1}
by construction of
\mathcal{R}
.
Thus we know the transition

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta_{1}\vDash_{s_{1}}\left<q_{1};\bot,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>\rightarrow\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\vDash_{s_{1}^{\prime}}\left<q_{1}^{\prime};\bot,\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime}\right>

can be concluded by some rule, and
(q_{1}^{\prime};\bot,\Delta_{1}^{\prime},s_{1}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{1}
.
Therefore, the transition

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta_{1}\vDash_{s_{1}}\left<q_{1};Q,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>\rightarrow\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\vDash_{s_{1}^{\prime}}\left<q_{1}^{\prime};Q,\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime}\right>

can be concluded by the same rule, and by construction of
\mathcal{R}
, we have that
(q_{1}^{\prime};Q,\Delta_{1}^{\prime},s_{1}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}
.

•

A special case of the above is if
q_{1}^{\prime}
in the reduct is empty.
Again we have that
(q_{1};\bot,\Delta_{1},s_{1})\in\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{1}
by construction of
\mathcal{R}
, and we know the transition

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta_{1}\vDash_{s_{1}}\left<q_{1};\bot,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>\rightarrow\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\vDash_{s_{1}^{\prime}}\left<\bot,\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime}\right>

can be concluded by some rule, and
(\bot,\Delta_{1}^{\prime},s_{1}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{1}
.
Still by construction of
\mathcal{R}
, we know that
\text{{fin}}(q_{1};\bot,\Delta_{1},s_{1})=(\Delta,s)
and, since the execution terminates, we have by Theorem
H.3
that
\Delta_{1}^{\prime}=\Delta
and
s_{1}^{\prime}=s
.
By the same reasoning as above, the transition

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta_{1}\vDash_{s_{1}}\left<q_{1};Q,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>\rightarrow\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s}\left<Q,\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime}\right>

can therefore be concluded and, since
(Q,\Delta,s)\in\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{2}
, we also have that
(Q,\Delta,s)\in\mathcal{R}
by construction.

•

Otherwise, the triplet
(Q_{1},\Delta_{1},s_{1})
came from
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{2}
.
Hence we know that the transition

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta_{1}\vDash_{s_{1}}\left<Q_{1},\text{env}_{TSV}\right>\rightarrow\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\vDash_{s_{1}^{\prime}}\left<Q_{1}^{\prime},\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime}\right>

can be concluded, and
(Q_{1}^{\prime},\Delta_{1}^{\prime},s_{1}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{2}
.
Therefore we also have that
(Q_{1}^{\prime},\Delta_{1}^{\prime},s_{1}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}
by construction.
∎

Notice that, in the construction of the candidate typing interpretation
\mathcal{R}
of the previous proof, we make use of two related facts:

•

Transitions only ever modify
the top
of the stack; hence, we can remove the
\bot
symbol from a stack
q;\bot
and suffix another stack
Q
onto it.

•

Theorem
H.3
tells us that,
if

q;\bot
eventually terminates normally when executed at level
s
and with type environment
\Delta
,
then
we can use the function
\text{{fin}}(\cdot)
to find the security level
s^{\prime}
and type environment
\Delta
in the terminal state.

By the definition of
\text{{fin}}(\cdot)
, we have that
\text{{fin}}(S;\bot,\Delta,s)=(\Delta,s)
, so in the construction of
\mathcal{R}
we make sure to only pick those triplets
(q;\bot,\Delta^{\prime},s^{\prime})
from
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{1}
for which it holds that
\text{{fin}}(q;\bot,\Delta^{\prime},s^{\prime})=(\Delta,s)
.
This ensures two things:

•

Every stack
q;\bot
that derives from the execution of
S
will be suffixed with
Q
and included in the candidate typing interpretation.

•

Only those stacks
q;\bot
that will terminate on
(\Delta,s)
, and hence are
compatible
with
Q
, will be suffixed with
Q
and included.
This ensures that the newly created stacks
q;Q
remain well-formed.

It is of course possible that some stacks
q;\bot
will not terminate, in which case Theorem
H.3
does not apply.
This is, however, not a problem, since the execution will then never reach the suffix
Q
in the compound stacks
q;Q
.
The predicate
\text{{fin}}(q;\bot,\Delta^{\prime},s^{\prime})=(\Delta,s)
still ensures that every compound stack
q;Q
is well-formed, and by Theorem
3.5
we know that well-formedness is preserved by the typed semantics, so the construction is still safe, even if the
Q
suffix is never reached.

Proof of Lemma
3.14
.

By the definition of
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta,x:\text{var}(B^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}});\text{env}_{T}\vDash\text{del}(x);Q:\text{cmd}(s)
, we must exhibit a typing interpretation
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{\prime}
which is such that

(\text{del}(x);Q,(\Delta,x:\text{var}(B^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}})),s)\in\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{\prime}.

From
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash Q:\text{cmd}(s)
, we know there exists a typing interpretation
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
containing the triplet
(Q,\Delta,s)
.
We then construct a candidate typing interpretation
\mathcal{R}
, such that it contains
(\text{del}(x);Q,(\Delta,x:\text{var}(B^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}})),s)
as follows:

\mathcal{R}\triangleq\left\{\,(\text{del}(x);Q,(\Delta,x:\text{var}(B^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}})),s)\,\right\}\cup\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}

As we have only added a single triple, all that now is needed is to show that there exists at least one transition

\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{T}\vDash(\text{del}(x);Q,(\Delta,x:\text{var}(B^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}})),s)\Mapsto P_{1}^{\prime}

and that, for all such transitions, the reduct is again in
\mathcal{R}
.

From
(Q,\Delta,s)\in\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
, we know that an appropriately shaped
\text{env}_{SV}
can be built from
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta
for all transitions from
Q
by the method in Definition
3.2
(and if
Q
has no transitions by Case
1
of Definition
3.8
, then the shape of
\text{env}_{SV}
is irrelevant).
Pick any such environment, and pick any value
v
such that
\text{{TypeOf}}_{\Gamma}(v)=(B_{1},s_{1})
and
\Sigma\vdash B_{1}\mathrel{<:}B^{\prime}
and
s_{1}\sqsubseteq s^{\prime}
.
Now, the transition

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta,x:\text{var}(B^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}})\vDash_{s}\left<\text{del}(x);Q,\text{env}_{TS};\text{env}_{V},x:v\right>\rightarrow\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s}\left<Q,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>

can be concluded by
and, by Corollary
3.4
, the transition can also be concluded for any other
\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime}
having the same structure and satisfying the requirements.
No other transition is possible, so we conclude that the transition

\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{T}\vDash(\text{del}(x);Q,(\Delta,x:\text{var}(B^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}})),s)\Mapsto(Q,\Delta,s)

exists and, as
(Q,\Delta,s)\in\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
, we also have that
(Q,\Delta,s)\in\mathcal{R}
by construction.
∎

Proof of Lemma

By the definition of
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash(\text{env}_{V}^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime});Q:\text{cmd}(s)

.

Proof of Lemma
3.17
.

By the definition of
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash\texttt{skip}:\text{cmd}(s)
, we must exhibit a typing interpretation
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
containing the triplet
(\texttt{skip};\bot,\Delta,s)
.
Here is our candidate typing interpretation:

\mathcal{R}\triangleq\left\{\,(\texttt{skip};\bot,\Delta,s),(\bot,\Delta,s)\,\right\}

and, clearly,
(\texttt{skip};\bot,\Delta,s)\in\mathcal{R}
.
We must now show that
\mathcal{R}
indeed is a typing interpretation by checking the criteria of Definition
3.8
.
We examine each of the pairs we have added:

•

By rule
we can conclude the transition

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s}\left<\texttt{skip};\bot,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>\rightarrow\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s}\left<\bot,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>

for any
\Delta
,
s
, and
\text{env}_{SV}
.
There are no other possible transitions, so we conclude that the transition

\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{T}\vDash(\texttt{skip};\bot,\Delta,s)\Mapsto(\bot,\Delta,s)

exists, and
(\bot,\Delta,s)\in\mathcal{R}
by construction.

•

The triplet
(\bot,\Delta,s)
belongs to
\mathcal{R}
by Case
1
of Definition
3.8
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.20
.

By the definition of
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash\texttt{if $e$ then $S_{\text{T}}$ else $S_{\text{F}}$}:\text{cmd}(s)
, we must exhibit a typing interpretation
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
which is such that it contains the triplet
(\texttt{if $e$ then $S_{\text{T}}$ else $S_{\text{F}}$};\bot,\Delta,s)
.

From
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash S_{\text{T}}:\text{cmd}(s)
and
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash S_{\text{F}}:\text{cmd}(s)
we know that there exist typing interpretations
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{2}
such that

\displaystyle(S_{\text{T}};\bot,\Delta,s)

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{1}

\displaystyle(S_{\text{F}};\bot,\Delta,s)

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{2}.

We then construct the following candidate typing interpretation, where
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
may be empty:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{\,(\texttt{if $e$ then $S_{\text{T}}$ else $S_{\text{F}}$};\bot,\Delta,s)\,\right\}

\displaystyle\cup\left\{\,(q_{1};s;\bot,\Delta_{1},s_{1})\mid(q_{1};\bot,\Delta_{1},s_{1})\in\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{1}\land\text{{fin}}(q_{1};\bot,\Delta_{1},s_{1})=(\Delta,s)\,\right\}

\displaystyle\cup\left\{\,(q_{2};s;\bot,\Delta_{2},s_{2})\mid(q_{2};\bot,\Delta_{2},s_{2})\in\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{2}\land\text{{fin}}(q_{2};\bot,\Delta_{2},s_{2})=(\Delta,s)\,\right\}

\displaystyle\cup\left\{\,(\bot,\Delta,s)\,\right\}

We must now show that
\mathcal{R}
in fact is a typing interpretation.
First we consider the two single triples that we added:

•

The triplet
(\bot,\Delta,s)
satisfies Case
1
of Definition
3.8
.

•

From
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash e:\text{bool}_{s}
and Definition
F.7
, we know that

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{\text{bool}_{s}}\left<e,\text{env}_{SV}\right>\rightarrow b

where
b
is a boolean of level
s
for any
\text{env}_{SV}
built from
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta
according to Definition
3.2
.
Thus, the transition

\displaystyle\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s}\left<\texttt{if $e$ then $S_{\text{T}}$ else $S_{\text{F}}$};\bot,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>\rightarrow\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s}\left<S_{b};s;\bot,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>

can be concluded by rule
, where
S_{b}
can be either
S_{\text{T}}
or
S_{\text{F}}
.
Hence, there are two possible transitions:

\displaystyle\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{T}

\displaystyle\vDash(\texttt{if $e$ then $S_{\text{T}}$ else $S_{\text{F}}$};\bot,\Delta,s)\Mapsto(S_{\text{T}};s;\bot,\Delta,s)

\displaystyle\Sigma;\Gamma;\text{env}_{T}

\displaystyle\vDash(\texttt{if $e$ then $S_{\text{T}}$ else $S_{\text{F}}$};\bot,\Delta,s)\Mapsto(S_{\text{F}};s;\bot,\Delta,s)

which by Corollary
3.4
can be concluded for any
\text{env}_{SV}
built from
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta
according to Definition
3.2
.
There are no other possible transitions.
Finally, since

\displaystyle(S_{\text{T}};\bot,\Delta,s)

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{1}\land\text{{fin}}(S_{\text{T}};\bot,\Delta,s)=(\Delta,s)

\displaystyle(S_{\text{F}};\bot,\Delta,s)

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{2}\land\text{{fin}}(S_{\text{F}};\bot,\Delta,s)=(\Delta,s)

we have that
(S_{\text{T}};s;\bot,\Delta,s)\in\mathcal{R}
and
(S_{\text{F}};s;\bot,\Delta,s)\in\mathcal{R}
by construction.

Next we consider an arbitrary triplet
(q_{1};s;\bot,\Delta_{1},s_{1})
from the set

\left\{\,(q_{1};s;\bot,\Delta_{1},s_{1}{\\
}(&~q_{1};\bot,\Delta_{1},s_{1})\in\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{1}\land\text{{fin}}(q_{1};\bot,\Delta_{1},s_{1})=(\Delta,s)\,\right\}.

(the case for triplets from the other set is similar).
As
q_{1}
can be empty, we have three cases to consider:

•

If
q_{1}
is empty, then it must have come from a triplet
(\bot,\Delta,s)\in\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{1}
, since
\text{{fin}}(\bot,\Delta,s)=(\Delta,s)
.
Thus, the triplet in
\mathcal{R}
is of the form
(s;\bot,\Delta,s)
by construction of
\mathcal{R}
.
As
s\sqsupseteq s
, this satisfies the side condition of
, so the transition

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s}\left<s;\bot,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>\rightarrow\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s}\left<\bot,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>

can be concluded by that rule for any appropriately shaped
\text{env}_{SV}
and
(\bot,\Delta,s)\in\mathcal{R}
by construction.

•

If
q_{1}
is of the form
\texttt{throw};q_{1}^{\prime}
, then this satisfies Case
1
of Definition
3.8
.

•

If
q_{1}
is non-empty and not of the form
\texttt{throw};q_{1}^{\prime}
, then we know is that
(q_{1};\bot,\Delta_{1},s_{1})\in\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{1}
, that
\text{{fin}}(q_{1},\Delta_{1},s_{1})=(\Delta,s)
, and that there exists at least one transition

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta_{1}\vDash_{s_{1}}\left<q_{1};\bot,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>\rightarrow\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\vDash_{s_{1}^{\prime}}\left<q_{1}^{\prime};\bot,\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime}\right>

for all appropriately shaped
\text{env}_{SV}
, with
(q_{1}^{\prime};\bot,\Delta_{1}^{\prime},s_{1}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{1}
.
There are now two possibilities, depending on the shape of
q_{1}^{\prime}
:

–

If
q_{1}^{\prime}
is empty, the reduct is of the form
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s}\left<\bot,\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime}\right>
.
Then, the transition

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta_{1}\vDash_{s_{1}}\left<q_{1};s;\bot,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>\rightarrow\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta\vDash_{s}\left<s;\bot,\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime}\right>

can likewise be concluded, and
(s;\bot,\Delta,s)\in\mathcal{R}
by construction.

–

Otherwise, if
q_{1}^{\prime}
is non-empty, then the reduct is instead of the form
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\vDash_{s_{1}^{\prime}}\left<q_{1}^{\prime};\bot,\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime}\right>
and
(q_{1}^{\prime};\bot,\Delta_{1}^{\prime},s_{1}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{1}
.
Then, the transition

\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta_{1}\vDash_{s_{1}}\left<q_{1};s;\bot,\text{env}_{TSV}\right>\rightarrow\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\vDash_{s_{1}^{\prime}}\left<q_{1}^{\prime};s;\bot,\text{env}_{T};\text{env}_{SV}^{\prime}\right>

can likewise be concluded.
Finally, by Theorem
H.3
, we have that
\text{{fin}}(q_{1}^{\prime};\bot,\Delta_{1}^{\prime},s_{1}^{\prime})=(\Delta,s)
, so by construction we also have that
(q_{1}^{\prime};s;\bot,\Delta_{1}^{\prime},s_{1}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.21
.

By the definition of
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash\texttt{while $e$ do $S$}:\text{cmd}(s)
, we must exhibit a typing interpretation
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}^{\prime}
containing the triplet
(\texttt{while $e$ do $S$};\bot,\Delta,s)
.

From
\Sigma;\Gamma;\Delta;\text{env}_{T}\vDash S:\text{cmd}(s)
we know that there exists a typing interpretation
\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
such that
(S,\Delta,s)\in\mathcal{R}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\text{env}_{T}}
.
We then construct the following candidate typing interpretation, where
q^{\prime}
may be empty:

Wemustnowshowthat
\mathcal{R}
infactisatypinginterpretation.Firstweconsiderthetwosingletripletsthatweadded:\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@The triplet $(\bot,\Delta,s)$ satisfies Case~\ref{case:typi1} of Definition~\ref{def:typing_interpretation_stm}.
